# Jan van Ruysbroeck <note>Jan van Ruysbroeck (1293–1381) was an influential Flemish mystic known as the "Admirable Doctor." He was a central figure in the movement of Dutch and German mysticism.</note>

## The Adornment of the Spiritual 
## Marriage <note>Original title: "Die Zierde der geistlichen Hochzeit." This is Ruysbroeck's most celebrated work, outlining the three stages of the spiritual life: the active, the yearning, and the contemplative.</note>

<note>Jan van Ruysbroeck (1293–1381) was one of the most influential Flemish mystics. This work, "The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage," is his masterpiece, detailing the soul's journey toward union with God through the active, interior, and contemplative lives.</note>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded the Table of Contents for the work, ending with the chapters of Book Three which focus on the "God-beholding life" and the renewal of the spirit. This page begins the translator's Preface, shifting from the formal structure of the book to a personal narrative about the origins of this specific edition.</meta>

**Preface.** Do not expect from me, dear friends and ac-
quaintances, a learned introduction to the blessed Jan
van Ruysbroeck’s most famous writing. Let me rather
tell you how this translation came about. In this way,
you will also learn much about him and his work,
but in a form that better corresponds to my nature and 
knowledge. » You have all likely had a childhood
friend from whom fate one day separated you, who 
then remained lost for years, until he suddenly 
reappeared and from then on played a major 
role in your life. » My relationship to the blessed Jan 
van Ruysbroeck, called the Admirable <note>original: "der Wunderbare"; Ruysbroeck is traditionally known by the Latin title Doctor Admirabilis due to the profound nature of his mystical writings</note>, on account 
of the high flight of his vision <note>original: "Schauens"; refers to the act of mystical contemplation or "beholding" God</note>, took a similar shape. 
It was in the year 1891, when I was twenty-three 
years old, that I first heard of him, and "The Adornment 
of the Spiritual Marriage" in the translation of the then 
still young Maeterlinck <note>Maurice Maeterlinck (1862–1949) was a Belgian Symbolist author who published a French translation of Ruysbroeck in 1891, helping to spark a modern revival of interest in the mystic</note> fell into my hands. I read 
it often, but understood little of the content. 
And yet I always felt strengthened and comforted 
when I laid the book aside, which is why I 
reached for it again and again. » I was, moreover, not 
at all surprised or ashamed that I understood little of 
Ruysbroeck’s teaching, for Maeterlinck had prepared 
me in his preface for something entirely sublime and 
at the same time entirely wild <note>original: "Tolles"; here suggesting something extraordinary or even maddeningly intense</note>. Ruysbroeck was said to be a 
drunken eagle, soaring above snow-covered mountain peaks. 
He combined the ignorance of a child with the 
knowledge <note>original: "Wissenschaft"; in this context, it refers to a deep, spiritual wisdom or experiential certainty rather than academic study</note> of a person who had risen from the dead. 
He also wrote like a child, but at the same time, pages that 

<summary>In this preface, the translator shares their personal journey with the works of the 14th-century mystic Jan van Ruysbroeck, describing how they first encountered his "Spiritual Marriage" through Maurice Maeterlinck's translation in 1891.</summary>
<keywords>Jan van Ruysbroeck, Maurice Maeterlinck, The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage, mysticism, preface, spiritual vision, Doctor Admirabilis</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the translator's preface, specifically finishing a summary of Maurice Maeterlinck’s assessment of Jan van Ruysbroeck before moving into the author's personal journey of rediscovering Ruysbroeck’s work between 1894 and 1917.</meta>

...Plato could have written. There was no order in his writings, he claimed, and all scholastic logic <note>the formal philosophical and logical system taught in medieval European universities</note> was missing. It was absolutely necessary to honestly warn the sluggish who were about to set foot upon the steps of this "temple without architecture." The first twenty chapters, even if perhaps necessary as an introduction, contained only overly sweet, sanctimonious platitudes... ❦ I kept the book until 1894, then I had to return it to the friend who had lent it to me, and with that, Ruysbroeck almost completely disappeared from my horizon. I did read around 1912 that the Benedictine Abbey of Wisques-Oosterhout was preparing a French edition and Dr. H. Moller a Dutch edition of the works of the Blessed <note>original: "des Seligen"; a title for a person who has been beatified by the Church</note>, but I did not learn that Fr. A. Lambert had already published a German translation of three of Ruysbroeck's writings, including "The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage," in the year 1903. ❦ When I stayed in my fatherland <note>the Netherlands</note> for a few weeks in 1917, I found a beautifully produced, thick book at a clergyman's house in Delft with the inscription: *Jan van Ruusbroec, the Admirable, Volume I* <note>original: "de Wonderbare"; this refers to Ruysbroeck's traditional epithet, "the Admirable"</note>. It was the first volume of the planned Dutch edition of Ruysbroeck’s works. I borrowed the book and now, after twenty-five years, read a portion of "The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage" once again. This time I understood much more of it, and the feeling of strengthening and nourishment after the reading was even stronger than before. Having returned to Beuron Abbey <note>a famous Benedictine monastery in Germany known for its scholarship and art</note>, I spoke to a Germanist <note>a scholar of Germanic languages and literature</note> who loved Ruysbroeck very much about that Dutch edition. <meta>This page continues the author's account of his personal journey with the writings of Jan van Ruysbroeck. Having rediscovered the Dutch mystic's work in 1917, he now describes how he moved from a casual reader to a dedicated translator within his monastic community.</meta>

...to order it for him. I had the book sent to me—but I kept it for myself and read from it occasionally. One day, I began to translate Ruysbroeck’s debut work, *The Kingdom of God’s Beloved*, following the nearly literal Dutch translation by Dr. Möller, so that I could present a finished work as a gift for my Abbot’s Name Day <note>The feast day of the saint after whom a monk is named; a significant day for celebration and gift-giving in a monastery</note>. I immediately felt the blessing of such work in prayer and in the service of God, and I translated away boldly, just as I used to read books in foreign languages in my youth, without using a dictionary and without worrying at first whether I understood everything correctly. I sought to immerse myself in my author <note>original: "Auktor"</note>, just as a child learns the meaning of the words it hears and thus gradually learns to speak and express itself. 

When this first task was finished, I tackled *The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage*, likewise initially following Dr. Möller’s Dutch text. By then, I had also obtained Lambert’s German translation, and in the library of the Beuron monastery <note>Beuron Archabbey in Germany, a famous center for the study of liturgy and theology</note>, I found the [incomplete] German edition of Ruysbroeck’s works by Gottfried Arnold, published in Offenbach am Main in the year 1701. This translation was based on the Latin version by the famous Carthusian <note>A member of a strictly contemplative religious order; the Latin translations by the Carthusians helped spread Ruysbroeck's work across Europe</note> Laurentius Surius (died 1522), which, though not literal, is nonetheless excellent. 

Gradually, I penetrated the understanding of Ruysbroeck’s teachings. Möller’s introduction contributed much to this, and Lambert’s translation also brought me greater clarity. It is precise, almost literal, but unfortunately somewhat dry and un—

...musical. Whether he could ever properly speak Dutch? He often fumbles about anxiously like a blind man and likely knew Catholic dogma only from books, not from personal experience. But Lambert performed pioneering work. He was the first to translate Ruysbroeck’s *The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage* and two smaller writings, *The Sparkling Stone* and the *Little Book of Supreme Truth* <note>original: "Büchlein von der höchsten Wahrheit"; often titled "The Book of Enlightenment" in English</note>, from the original text into German. This distinction cannot be taken away from him.

I had barely completed the first version of *The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage* when a beautifully bound edition of this work, translated by Friedrich Markus Huebner, was published by Insel-Verlag <note>A prestigious German publishing house known for its high aesthetic standards and "Insel-Bücherei" series</note>. Now I began my work anew, comparing the individual texts with my own translation. In Huebner’s version, I often found a better word or the occasional better sentence, but also passages that are clearly incorrect, though not to the same degree or in such large numbers as in his *Twelve Beguines*. <meta>This page continues the author’s defense of Jan van Ruysbroeck’s writing style against the criticisms of Maurice Maeterlinck. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing Maeterlinck’s inability to see the structural order in Ruysbroeck’s work.</meta>

...the material of this masterpiece with the architectural structure of a Gothic cathedral. Certainly, the subject matter that Ruysbroeck treats is often difficult, but his presentation is simple and clear. One must work one’s way into the material, just as one does with geometry or any other subject. One must choose the Blessed one <note>original: "den Seligen"; referring to Jan van Ruysbroeck, who was beatified in 1908</note> as one’s master, feel oneself to be a student, and receive his words in humility, reflecting upon and contemplating them. And once one has read through ***The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage*** <note>Ruysbroeck’s most famous work, also known as *The Spiritual Espousals*</note>, has gained an overview of the material, and noted the division into chapters (which the table of contents is intended to facilitate), then one must begin reading again from the start. Above all, one must put into practice what the master teaches in the first book of his primary work. This work by no means contains only "saccharine pieties," as Maeterlinck claims, but rather deals with the active or foundational life, "which is necessary for all people who wish to be saved."

For those who are familiar with the writings of Saint Teresa <note>Saint Teresa of Avila (1515–1582), a Spanish mystic and Doctor of the Church known for her writings on the soul's journey to God</note>, it will be a joy to compare her teaching on the four stages of prayer with Ruysbroeck’s explanations. They will find in the twenty-fifth chapter of the first book an introduction to the first stage of prayer, to contemplative prayer. In the eighteenth chapter of the second book, the second stage of prayer, the **prayer of quiet**, is described, after the **prayer of recollection** had already been discussed in the ninth chapter. The Blessed Jan van Ruysbroeck <note>Jan van Ruysbroeck (1293–1381) was a Flemish mystic known as the "Doctor Ecstaticus" for his profound writings on the soul's union with God.</note>, called "the Wonderful" <note>original: "der Wunderbare"</note>, was born in 1293 in the small village of Ruysbroeck, not far from Brussels. At the age of eleven, he began his studies with his uncle, Master Hinckaert, a canon <note>A member of the clergy serving a cathedral or collegiate church</note> at St. Gudula in Brussels. From this relative, he evidently received a thorough theological education and was ordained as a priest at twenty-four. Until his fiftieth year, he served as a simple secular priest <note>A priest who lives in the world rather than in a monastic community</note> at St. Gudula; then, he withdrew from the world with a few friends and founded the Groenendael Monastery in the Forest of Soignes. In the year 1350, this community adopted the rule of the Augustinian Canons Regular <note>A religious order of priests living in community under the Rule of Saint Augustine</note>, and Ruysbroeck became its prior following the death of his friend and fellow brother, Franco van Coudenberg. He died in the year 1381. The veneration of the "Blessed" Jan van Ruysbroeck, which had existed since time immemorial, was officially recognized by a decree of the Congregation of Rites <note>The Vatican body formerly responsible for the process of beatification and canonization</note> on December 1, 1908. — What is truly beautiful about the saints is that they do not wish to be anything "extraordinary" <note>original: "Absonderliches"</note> but rather "wish to be like other people," that they are "internally so rich and overflowing with good teachings," and that they possess "an all-encompassing love that flows out into heaven and onto earth." <meta>This page continues the introduction to the life and character of Jan van Ruysbroeck, transitioning from his biographical details to his nature as a "universal" mystic and providing guidance on the specific terminology used in his writings.</meta>

Universal souls—with them, it is never merely the feelings or the intellect that speak, but always the whole person. They are often not scholars and yet they teach the highest wisdom; they are rarely poets and yet their work is full of poetry; they are not moralists and yet they lead us toward the highest virtue. Ruysbroeck is just such a "whole person," the "Wonderful" <note>original: "der Wunderbare"</note> among the mystics. You will, my dear readers, come to love him if you often immerse yourselves in his book. He himself considered *The Ornament of the Spiritual Marriage* <note>original: "Zierde der geistlichen Hochzeit". This is Ruysbroeck's most influential work, describing the soul's journey toward God through the active, yearning, and contemplative lives.</note> to be his masterpiece; he had it copied several times and sent it to his German friends. Now he sends it to you through me, his latest pupil, so that you may become "good" people, or even "inward" ones—indeed, "God-beholding," if God wills it. )) <note>The double closing parentheses appear in the original text, perhaps marking the end of a specific introductory thought.</note>

Now I must give you a few hints. Do not be put off by certain words like "mode" <term>Weise: In Ruysbroeck's vocabulary, this refers to the 'way' or 'manner' of human action or being.</term> and "modelessness" <term>Unweise: A technical term for the state of being beyond human ways, specifically the 'wayless' way of God's own being.</term>, "unattached" <term>Ledig: Literally 'empty' or 'single,' meaning a soul free from worldly attachments and mental images.</term> and "attached," or "unity" and "multiplicity." Like hunters, mystics have their own language. Anyone who engages with mysticism must learn it. I have found again and again that most translators of mystical writings initially try to paraphrase such technical terms or seek to avoid them. By the end of their work, however, they use them all. Therefore, I have preferred to introduce them right from the start. I hope soon to be able to complete a short introduction to Ruysbroeck’s teachings, though it will only bring light to those who have already occupied themselves with our Blessed [author]. There, many a technical term will also be explained. Then I ask you, be

<meta>This page marks the transition from the modern translator’s introductory remarks (completed at Beuron in 1922) to the actual text of Jan van Ruysbroeck’s 14th-century masterpiece. # Here begins the first book of the Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage. <note>This work, originally written in Middle Dutch as *Die Geestelike Brulocht*, is often referred to in English as *The Spiritual Espousals*. The word "Adornment" (Zierde) refers to the spiritual preparation and virtues that "decorate" the soul for its union with God.</note>

**Preface.** "Behold, the bridegroom comes, go out to meet him." These words are reported to us by Saint Matthew the Evangelist <note>Matthew 25:6, from the Parable of the Ten Virgins.</note>, and Christ spoke them to his disciples and to all people in the parable of the virgins. This bridegroom is Christ, and human nature is the bride, whom God created in his own image and likeness. And he had placed her in the beginning in the most exalted, most beautiful, richest, and most fertile spot on earth, namely in Paradise. And he had subjected all creatures to her and had adorned her with graces; and he had given her a commandment so that, through obedience, she might have earned the right to be firmly united with her bridegroom in eternal loyalty, without ever falling into any hardship or sin. » Then came a deceiver, the hellish enemy (who was envious of this) in the form of a cunning serpent and deceived the woman; and both deceived the man, the primary bearer <term>Hauptträger: literally the "head-bearer" or principal representative, referring to Adam as the head of the human race</term> of human nature. And with deceptive counsel, he robbed nature—the bride of God; and she was driven out into a foreign land, poor and miserable, imprisoned, oppressed, and made a slave by her enemies, as if she ne— <note>The text breaks off mid-word here ("nie-") and continues on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the primary scriptural theme of the book, Matthew 25:6, and establishes the allegorical framework of the "Spiritual Marriage" between Christ and humanity, beginning with the Fall of Man in Paradise.</summary>
<keywords>Bridegroom, Bride, Christ, Human Nature, Paradise, The Fall, Serpent, Adam and Eve, Spiritual Marriage, Jan van Ruysbroeck</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Preface of the first book. It completes the sentence from the previous page: the soul, having been deceived and enslaved by the "hellish enemy," was left in a state where she could never again return to her homeland and find atonement through her own power.</meta>

...could never again return to her homeland and find atonement. » But when it seemed the right time to God, and he had compassion for the suffering of his beloved, he sent his only-begotten Son down to earth into a rich hall and a glorious temple—that was the body of the Virgin Mary. There he wedded himself to that bride, our nature, and united her with his person through the purest blood of the noble Virgin. The priest who married the bride was the Holy Spirit; the Angel Gabriel announced the marriage; the glorious Virgin gave her consent. 

Thus Christ, our faithful bridegroom, has united our nature with himself and has sought us out in the foreign land; he has instructed us in heavenly customs and in perfect faithfulness. He has worked and fought like a champion <term>Kämpe: a medieval term for a knightly fighter or combatant in a duel</term> against our enemies; he has broken open the prison and won the battle, and he has killed our death with his own death. He has redeemed us with his blood and liberated us in baptism with his water, making us rich through his sacraments and his gifts: so that we may "go out" with all virtues (as he says) and go to meet him in the hall of glory, to enjoy him without end for eternity. 

» Now speaks the Master of Truth, Christ: "Behold, the bridegroom comes, go out to meet him." <note>original: "Sehet, der Bräutigam kommt, gehet aus, ihm entgegen." <meta>This page continues the breakdown of the verse "Behold, the bridegroom comes, go out to meet him" (Matthew 25:6) and introduces the three-part structure of the treatise: the active life, the interior life, and the contemplative life.</meta>

with the second word, he shows us what we should see, namely the arrival of the bridegroom, by saying: "The bridegroom comes." In the third place, he teaches and commands us what we should do, by saying: "Go out." In the fourth place, when he says: "To meet him," he instructs us concerning the reward and purpose of all our labor and our whole life, namely the loving encounter with the bridegroom. » We wish to explain and set forth these words in three ways: First, in the common way regarding an elementary or active life <term>tätiges Leben: the life of outward service, moral discipline, and good works</term>, as is necessary for all people who wish to be saved. Second, we want to present these same words regarding an inward, elevated, and God-desiring life, which many people reach through virtue and God's grace. Third, we want to explain them regarding a superessential <note>original: "überwesentliches." This refers to a state of being beyond ordinary human essence, existing in union with God.</note>, God-beholding life, which only a few people can achieve and taste because of the height and nobility of this life.

## 1. Here begins the "active life."

Now, then, to the first point. Christ, the Wisdom of the Father, speaks and has spoken inwardly, according to his divinity, to all people from the time of Adam: "Behold." And there is a need for seeing. Now, take serious note: for one who is to see, whether physically or spiritually, three things are necessary. » First, to see physically from the outside, a person needs the external light of heaven or some other material light, so that the medium <note>In medieval philosophy, the "medium" refers to the space or substance (like air) through which light and images travel to the eye.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the requirements for sight, transitioning from physical sight to the spiritual requirements for the "active life." It begins mid-sentence, completing the three requirements for physical sight established on the previous page.</meta>

...through which one must see—in this case, the air—must be illuminated. Second, the willingness to allow the things one is meant to see to be reflected in one's eyes. Third, the instruments, the eyes, must be healthy and without blemish, so that coarse, physical things can be reflected in them in the finest way. If a person lacks any of these three, physical sight does not succeed.

We will speak no more of this physical sight, but rather of a spiritual and supernatural sight, in which our entire salvation <note>original: "Seligkeit." This term encompasses both ultimate happiness and the state of being saved.</note> lies. ☽ For this spiritual and supernatural sight, three things are likewise required. First, the light of God's grace; second, a will freely turned [toward God]; third, a conscience pure of mortal sins. ☽ So, take heed.

Because God is a universal good and his unfathomable love <term>Minne: a Middle High German and Middle Dutch term for divine, mystical love</term> is common to all, he bestows his grace in two ways: as prevenient grace <term>zuvorkommende Gnade: from the Latin "gratia praeveniens," this is the grace that "comes before" human action, reaching out to the soul before it even seeks God</term> and as the grace through which one earns eternal life.

Prevenient grace is common to all people—pagans and Jews, the good and the evil. Because of his universal love, which God holds for all people, he has caused his name and the redemption of human nature to be preached and revealed to all ends of the earth. Whoever wishes to convert can convert. All the sacraments, both that of Baptism and the others, stand ready for all people who wish to receive them, each according to his need; for God wills to save all people and destroy no one. On the Day of Judgment, no one will...

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the requirements for receiving divine grace. While the previous page introduced the metaphor of grafting a fruitful shoot onto a wild tree, this page elaborates on the first of the three requirements: "prevenient grace," explaining how God initiates the process of conversion through both external circumstances and internal contemplation.</meta>

Turning toward God and the purification of the conscience—not all people achieve these; and therefore the grace of God remains withheld from them, through which they would earn eternal life.

Prevenient grace <term>zuvorkommende Gnade: from the Latin 'gratia praeveniens', this refers to the divine influence that precedes any human decision or merit, "nudging" the soul toward God</term> touches a person from without and from within. From without, it may occur through illnesses or the loss of external goods, dear relatives and friends, or through public shame. Or one may be seized by preaching, or by the good examples of the saints or virtuous people, moved by their words and works so that one arrives at self-knowledge <note>Self-knowledge (Selbsterkenntnis) was a cornerstone of medieval mysticism; it was believed that by looking deeply into one's own soul, one would eventually find the image of God.</note>. This is God's influence from without.

At times, a person is also touched from within while contemplating the pains and sufferings of our Lord and all the goodness that God has shown to him and to all people. Or perhaps when he envisions his sins, the shortness of life, the fear of death and hell, the eternal torments of hell, and the eternal joys of heaven, and considers how God has spared him in his sins and waits for his conversion. Or he contemplates the wonder-work that God has created: heaven and earth and all creatures. These are the effects of the prevenient grace of God, which move a person from without and from within in manifold ways.

Furthermore, the human being has a natural inclination toward God by virtue of the "spark of the soul" <term>Seelenfunke: a key term in Rhineland Mysticism (associated with Meister Eckhart and Johannes Tauler) referring to an unquenchable, divine core within the human spirit that remains directed toward the Creator</term> and the highest reason, which always desires the good and hates the evil. In this way, God touches people according to the need and plight of the individual, such that the person is thereby struck <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the author likely continues to describe the soul being "struck" with remorse or a desire for God.</note>

<summary>This page explains "prevenient grace" as a divine intervention that reaches the soul through external hardships, like illness, and internal reflections, such as the contemplation of mortality. It introduces the mystical concept of the "spark of the soul" as an innate human faculty that naturally seeks the divine.</summary>

For in this light, God gives Himself out of free goodness and generosity, which no creature can earn before it is granted to them. And this is a secret working of God upon the soul, beyond time, and it moves the soul together with all its powers. Here, the activity of prevenient grace ceases, and the second grace—the supernatural light—begins.

### 2. That we should distinguish three kinds of the coming of Christ.

Now He further teaches what one should see, in that He speaks: "The Bridegroom comes." Christ, our Bridegroom, says this word in English: "He comes" <note>original: "Venit"; in Latin, this word can mean both "He comes" (present tense) and "He has come" (perfect tense).</note>. The word encompasses within itself two times: the time that is past, and the time that is now present; nevertheless, He means here the time that is still to come. 𝕯 And thus we should distinguish a threefold coming of our Bridegroom, Jesus Christ. In the first coming, He became man for the sake of humanity, out of love. The second coming occurs daily, often and frequently in every loving heart, with new graces and gifts, according to how the person is receptive to them. In the third coming, one beholds the arrival of Christ for judgment or in the hour of death.

<keywords>repentance, confession, satisfaction, divine seeing, Bridegroom, three comings, Advent, grace, judgment</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from page 18, which introduced the three-fold coming of Christ, this page begins to analyze the first coming—the Incarnation—by examining its underlying causes, Christ's internal state, and His external actions.</meta>

In every coming, three things are to be observed: the cause or the "why," the inner process, and the outward works.

☽ The cause why God created the angels and humanity was His unfathomable goodness and nobility; He desired that the bliss and richness which He Himself is should be revealed to rational creatures, so that they might taste Him in time and, beyond time, enjoy Him in eternity.

☽ The reason why God became man was His incomprehensible love and the distress of all people; for they had been corrupted since the <term>Fall of Man <note>original: Sündenfall; refers to the biblical story of humanity's initial turn away from God, resulting in a state of spiritual separation</note></term> and were unable to make amends for it themselves.

☽ But the cause why Christ, according to both His divinity and His humanity, performed all His works on earth is fourfold: His divine, immeasurable love; the created love, called <term>Charity <note>original: Caritas; in this context, it refers to the highest form of love, a selfless and divine grace</note></term>, which filled His soul by virtue of the union with the eternal Word and as a perfect gift from His Father; the great distress of human nature; and finally, the glory of His Father. These are the causes of the coming of Christ, our Bridegroom, and of all His works, both outward and inward.

☽ Now we must observe in Christ our Bridegroom—so that we may be spurred on to follow Him in the virtues according to the measure of our strength—the manner He maintained inwardly and the works He performed outwardly: His virtues and His virtuous deeds.

☽ The [mode of being] which He had according to His divinity is inaccessible and incomprehensible to us, for that consists in being born without ceasing from the Father, and that

Those virtues and those manners in the soul of Christ far surpassed the understanding and comprehension of all creatures. But we choose three of them: namely, humility, love, and suffering—or endurance—both inwardly and outwardly in patience. These are the three main roots and beginnings of all virtues and all perfection.

### 4. **Concerning the Love of Christ, Adorned with All Virtues.**

The second characteristic was love, the beginning and origin of all virtues. This love held the highest powers of the soul in a stillness and in the enjoyment of the same <term>blessedness <note>original: Seligkeit; referring to the state of perfect happiness or beatitude found in God</note></term> that he now enjoys in heaven. And this same love held him without ceasing directed toward his Father in awe, in tenderness, in praise and adoration, in heartfelt prayer for the needs of all people, and in the offering up of all his works to the honor of his Father.

¶ Also, this same love caused Christ to bend down in <term>loving loyalty <note>original: minniglicher Treue; "minniglich" is a Middle High German term for a deep, spiritual, and affectionate love</note></term> and grace toward all people’s physical and spiritual needs. And thus, through his life, he gave all people an example of how they should live. He fed all people who were receptive to it from within, spiritually with true doctrine, and from without through the senses with miracles and signs; at times, he also fed them with physical food when they followed him into the wilderness and could not go without nourishment. He made the deaf hear and let the crippled walk upright; he made the blind see and the mute speak, and he cast the <term>evil enemy <note>original: böser Feind; a common historical reference to the Devil or demonic spirits</note></term> out of people. He raised the dead to life; this should be understood both physically and spiritually. Christ, our lover, has labored for us from without and from within, in true loyalty. We cannot understand his love to its very depths; for it flowed from the bottomless source of the Holy Spirit, more than in any creatures who ever received love; for he

## 5. Concerning Christ’s Patient Suffering even unto Death.

The third characteristic is suffering in patience. This we must contemplate with zeal, for it adorns Christ, our <term>bridegroom <note>original: Bräutigam; a standard mystical term representing the union between Christ and the soul</note></term>, throughout his entire life. He began to suffer early on; barely was he born when he suffered poverty and cold. He was circumcised and shed his blood. He was brought to safety in a foreign land <note>referring to the Flight into Egypt to escape King Herod</note>. He served Saint Joseph and his mother. He suffered hunger and thirst, shame and mockery, and endured dishonorable words and deeds from the <term>Jews <note>In the context of medieval devotional literature, this often refers specifically to the religious authorities and crowds described in the Gospel accounts of the Passion</note></term>. He fasted, kept vigil, and was tempted by the enemy <note>referring to the Devil in the wilderness</note>. He was subject to all people. He traveled from land to land, from city to city, with much labor and great zeal, to preach the Gospel. Finally, he was taken captive by the Jews who were his enemies, though he was their friend. He was betrayed, mocked, and ridiculed, scourged and beaten, and condemned upon false testimony. He carried his cross in great pain to the highest place on earth <note>Golgotha or Calvary</note>. He was stripped and made bare. Never did man nor woman see such a beautiful body so mistreated. He suffered shame, pain, and cold before all the world, for he was naked, and it was cold, and the sharp wind drove through his wounds. He was nailed to the <term>wood of the cross <note>original: Kreuzholz</note></term> with coarse nails and stretched out so that his veins were torn. He was

## 8. Of the third coming of Christ.

The third coming of Christ, which is still hidden in the future, takes place at the Judgment or in the hour of death. Its more immediate occasion consists of: the appropriate time, the corresponding cause, and the justice of the Judge. ☽ The appropriate time for this coming is the hour of death and the final judgment over all people. When God created the soul out of nothing and united it with the body, He appointed for it a specific day and a specific hour—known to Him alone—when it must leave this temporal world and appear before His face. ☽ The corresponding cause—for the soul must answer before the eternal Truth and give an account of everything it has ever spoken and ever done in its life. ☽ The justice of the Judge—for the judgment and the passing of the sentence belongs to Christ. He is the <term>Son of Man <note>A title for Jesus that emphasizes both His humanity and His role as the divinely appointed judge of the world.</note></term> and the wisdom of the Father. Before this wisdom, everything...

### 9. How Christ will conduct himself at the Last Judgment. 

Christ our <term>Bridegroom <note>A central image in this text, portraying Christ as the lover and partner of the human soul.</note></term> and Judge will reward and punish according to justice at this judgment, for he repays everyone according to merit. He grants the righteous an immeasurable reward for every work offered to God—a reward that no <term>creature <note>In this context, 'creature' (Kreatur) refers to any created being, specifically human beings, as distinct from the Creator.</note></term> can earn by its own power—namely, himself. Because God works alongside the creature in these deeds, the creature earns, through the power of God, God himself as a reward. 

And with due justice, he delivers the damned to eternal woe and eternal pain, because they disdained and rejected an eternal good for the sake of a fleeting, temporary good. Voluntarily, contrary to his honor and his will, they turned away from God and toward created things. Thus, they are justly condemned. ☽ Those who bear witness at the judgment are the angels and the consciences of men. The accuser is the hellish enemy, and the judge is Christ, whom no one can deceive.

## II. Concerning a Spiritual Going Out Toward All Virtues.

Now understand and reflect. Christ begins his address with the word “Behold,” and this occurs—as you heard at the beginning—through love and a pure conscience. Now he has also shown us what we must see, namely this threefold coming. 

Now he commands us what we should do next and says: “Go out.” If you have fulfilled the first part and are seeing through grace and love, and if you have rightly and well contemplated your model, Christ, and his departure <note>In this context, "departure" (original: "Ausgang") refers to Christ "going out" from the Father to become human, or his selfless "going out" of himself in love on the cross.</note>, then there awakens in you—as a fruit of love and through the <term>tender <note>original: "minnigliche". This term is rooted in the medieval German concept of "Minne"—a profound, affectionate, and often ecstatic love between the soul and God.</note></term> contemplation of your Bridegroom—a righteous desire to follow him in the virtues. Then Christ speaks within you: “Go out.” This going out must happen in a threefold way. We must go out toward God, toward ourselves, and toward our fellow Christians, and this with works of love and justice. For love always strives upward toward the kingdom of God, which is God himself, for he is the source from which it flowed directly, and in which it remains permanently through union. Justice, which is born from love, desires to observe all customs and virtues, which...

Love and justice: these two lay a foundation in the kingdom of the soul, in which God shall make His dwelling; and this foundation is humility. These three virtues bear the entire weight and the whole edifice of the virtues and all things noble. 

For Love constantly holds before man’s eyes the bottomless goodness of God, from which she flows, so that he may live honorably before the face of God, remain steadfast, and grow in all virtues and in true humility. Justice, in turn, holds before man the eternal truth of God, so that the truth may not remain hidden from him, and he may be enlightened and fulfill all virtues without straying. Humility, however, constantly keeps the high omnipotence of God before man’s eyes, so that he may always remain small and modest, relying on God and not building upon himself. This is the way in which man should walk before God, so that he may always grow in new virtues.

## 13. On Obedience.

From this humility, obedience arises, for no one but the humble can be inwardly obedient. <note>The letter "D" in the source text likely serves as a decorative paragraph marker or initials a new section.</note> Obedience is a humble, submissive, and compliant state of mind, and a willing readiness for all good things. It makes the person subject to the commandments, the prohibitions, and the will of God, and subjects the senses and the sensory powers to the supreme <term>reason <note>original: "Vernunft." In this context, it refers to the higher intellectual faculty that governs animal instincts and desires.</note>,</term> so that the person lives properly and according to reason. It also makes the person subject and obedient to the Holy Church, its sacraments and superiors, its teachings, commandments, and counsels, and all good customs practiced within holy <term>Christendom <note>original: "Christenheit."</note>.</term>

Obedience also makes a person willing and compliant in assisting their equals through counsel, deed, and service—both physical and spiritual—as each requires and as wise discernment suggests. <note>Another "D" marker appears here in the original text.</note> And it drives away disobedience, which is an offspring of <term>pride <note>original: "Hoffart." A traditional term for arrogance or vanity, considered the root of all sins.</note></term> and which should be shunned more than poison. Obedience in will and deeds adorns, perfects, and reveals a person’s humility. It establishes peace within communities. If it dwells in the superior, as is fitting, it also attracts their subordinates and maintains peace and harmony among equals. Finally, whoever [practices] obedience—

## 14. On the Denial of One's Own Will.

From this obedience arises the denial of one’s own will and one’s own <term>judgment <note>original: "Gutdünken." Refers to one's own opinion or self-directed discretion.</note>,</term> for no one can subordinate their own will to the will of another in all things except for the obedient person; for one can perform outward works and yet remain self-willed. Denial of one's own will ensures that a person lives without doing or omitting anything that would be peculiar or foreign to the saints, their teaching, and their lives; and it causes him to act always for the glory of God, according to His commandments and according to the will of his superiors, and to considerately preserve the peace of all those with whom he lives, as far as justice and prudence require. 

<note>The letter "D" in the source text serves as a decorative paragraph marker or initial.</note> Through the denial of one’s own will in doing, in refraining, and in suffering, the material and opportunity for <term>pride <note>original: "Hoffart." A traditional term for arrogance, often seen as the root of all sin.</note></term> are entirely removed, and humility is perfected to the highest degree. And God's will becomes entirely master over the person, and the human will is so intimately united with God’s will that he can neither want nor desire anything else. 

Such a person has put off the <term>old man <note>original: "alten Menschen." A Pauline biblical concept referring to the unregenerate, sinful nature (Ephesians 4:22-24).</note></term> and put on the new, who is renewed and created according to the most beloved will of God. Of him, Christ says: “Blessed are the poor in spirit”—that is, those who [deny] their own will...

### 15. On Patience.
From the <term>releasement <note>original: "Gelassenheit." A key term in German mysticism referring to a state of letting go of one's ego and self-will to be fully open to God.</note></term> of the will, patience arises; for no one can be perfectly patient in all things except for the person who has subordinated their own will to the will of God, as well as to the will of other people, wherever it is beneficial and appropriate. Patience is a quiet enduring of everything that can break upon a person, whether from God or from His creatures. Nothing is able to disturb the patient person: no loss of earthly goods, friends, or relatives; no sickness or shame; neither life nor death, nor <term>purgatory <note>original: "Fegfeuer."</note>,</term> nor the devil, nor hell; for he has entrusted himself in true love to the will of God. Since he is not weighed down by <term>mortal sins <note>original: "Todsünden." In traditional theology, these are grave sins that sever the soul's relationship with God.</note>,</term> he finds everything easy that God imposes upon him in time and eternity. This patience adorns a person and armors him against anger, a hot-tempered nature, and impatience in suffering—things by which a person is often disturbed, both inwardly and outwardly, and through which the ground is prepared for manifold <term>temptations <note>original: "Versuchungen."</note>.</term>

## 17. On Goodness.

From the same source as meekness, goodness springs forth, for no one can be truly good except the one who is meek. This goodness brings it about that a person shows a friendly face toward angry people, gives a loving answer, and performs every possible act of kindness for them, as long as he can hope that they will come to their senses and improve. Through forbearance and goodness, love remains alive and fruitful within a person; for a heart full of goodness resembles a lamp full of noble oil. The oil of goodness shines before the straying sinner with good examples and anoints and heals with comforting words and deeds those who are wounded in heart, sorrowful, or agitated. And it burns and shines brightly in the fire of love; for those who live in virtue, and

### **18. On Compassion.**

From goodness springs <term>compassion <note>original: "Mitleiden." Literally "suffering-with," this refers to the empathetic sharing of another's pain.</note></term> and a general concern for all people, for no one can suffer with all people except for the person who is good. "Compassion is an inner movement of the heart in mercy for the physical and spiritual needs of all people." This compassion causes a person to suffer and endure in union with <term>Christ’s Passion <note>original: "Christi Leiden." The historical and theological term for the suffering of Jesus leading up to and during his crucifixion.</note></term>, when he considers the cause and the manner of his suffering and his <term>composure <note>original: "Gelassenheit." In the tradition of German mysticism, this refers to a state of detachment, equanimity, and surrender to the will of God.</note></term>, his love, his wounds, his tenderness, the pains, the shame, his magnanimity, his misery, the disgrace and ignominy, the crown, the nails, all his goodness, his ruin, and death in patience. This unheard-of, manifold pain of Christ, our Savior and Bridegroom, moves the good person to compassion and mercy for Christ.

Through compassion, the person enters into himself and his eyes are opened to his own failings, to his deficient progress in the virtues and his small zeal for the honor of God, to his <term>lukewarmness <note>A common spiritual term for a state of indifference or lack of religious fervor.</note></term> and laziness, to the multitude of his faults and the waste of his time; and he sees that even now he still has little virtue, and his moral conduct is still imperfect. All this causes a person to have sincere compassion for himself as well. Furthermore, compassion leads to the recognition of others' errors and wanderings; how greatly people neglect their God and their <term>salvation <note>original: "Seligkeit." Refers to eternal happiness or the state of being saved.</note></term>...

<summary>This page defines compassion as a natural result of goodness, describing it as both an empathetic union with Christ's suffering and a tool for honest self-examination. It argues that true compassion allows a person to recognize their own spiritual flaws and the spiritual needs of others.</summary>
<keywords>compassion, Christ's Passion, Gelassenheit, spiritual growth, mercy, self-reflection, salvation</keywords>

...negligent, seeing how ungrateful they are for all the good that God has shown them, and for all the pains he suffered for their sake; how poorly they know <term>virtue <note>original: "Tugend." This can mean both blessedness in this life and the ultimate state of being in heaven.</note></term>. This observation awakens great compassion in the good person, in concern for the salvation of all people.

D A person should also have a merciful eye for the physical distress of their fellow Christians and for the many sufferings of human nature, such as when they see people's hunger, thirst, cold, nakedness, illness, poverty, and contempt, as well as the manifold distress of the poor, the grief over the loss of relatives and friends, of property, of honor and peace, and all the countless hardships to which human nature is subject. This moves the good-hearted person to compassion, and they suffer with everyone. But what pains them most is that people are so impatient in these trials, losing their reward and only too often deserving hell. Such is the work of compassion and mercy. 

D This work of compassion and all-encompassing love overcomes and drives away the third <term>deadly sin <note>original: "Hauptsünde." Literally "head sin." In traditional theology, these are the primary vices that lead to other sins.</note></term>, namely hatred and envy. For compassion is a wound of the heart that causes love to overflow onto all people. And this wound cannot heal as long as virtue still lives in the person; for God has to the heart...

### 19. On Charity.

Out of this mercy flows <term>charity <note>original: "Mildtätigkeit." While it can mean modern almsgiving, here it refers to the broader theological virtue of "Caritas"—a selfless, divinely inspired love for others.</note></term>, for no one can be supernaturally charitable with all-encompassing, heartfelt benevolence except for the merciful person. This is true even though one might give generously to specific individuals they are fond of without possessing divine love and supernatural <term>beneficence <note>original: "Wohltätigkeit." The author distinguishes between human kindness (giving to friends) and spiritual charity (giving out of love for God).</note></term>.

» Charity is the generous outpouring of a heart that is moved by love and mercy. When a person compassionately contemplates the suffering and the <term>agony of Christ <note>original: "Pein Christi." This refers to the physical and spiritual torture endured by Jesus during the Passion.</note></term>, there arises from it a generosity that leads them to offer Christ praise, thanks, honor, and veneration for His pain and love, and to give themselves to Him in joyful and humble submission, body and soul, in time and eternity. 

And when a person then looks upon themselves with compassion and takes pity on their own state—considering God’s blessings and their own <term>shortcomings <note>original: "Unzulänglichkeit." Refers to the human inability to achieve holiness without divine aid.</note></term>—they must take refuge in the charity of God, in His forbearance, faithfulness, and reliability, ready at all times to serve Him with a perfect, free will. The charitable person, who sees the errors, wanderings, and injustices of others, desires and

In this context, it refers to the external practice of the internal virtue of mercy.</note></term>, one practices the <term>seven works of mercy <note>The "Seven Corporal Works of Mercy" are traditional Christian acts of kindness to help others' physical needs, such as feeding the hungry and visiting the sick.</note></term>—the rich by providing assistance with their possessions and wealth, and the poor with goodwill and sincere benevolence, sustained by the desire to do the same if they had the means. In this way, the virtue of <term>generosity <note>original: "Freigebigkeit." A virtue focused on the liberal sharing of resources without attachment to material things.</note></term> is fulfilled. ❦ Through charity of the spirit, all virtues are multiplied and all powers of the soul are adorned. For the charitable person is always of a cheerful mind and an unburdened heart, overflowing with benevolence and concerned for all people in the practice of virtue. For whoever is charitable and does not cling to earthly things is like God, no matter how poor they may be; for their entire inner self and all their feeling is an outpouring and a giving. In this way, they have driven out the fourth <term>capital sin <note>Also known as the "Seven Deadly Sins," these were considered the roots of all other moral failings.</note></term>, <term>avarice and greed <note>original: "Geiz und Habsucht." The author distinguishes between "Geiz" (stinginess or withholding) and "Habsucht" (the craving to acquire more).</note></term>. Of such a person, Christ says:

> “Blessed are the merciful, for they shall obtain mercy,” <note>original: "Selig sind die Barmherzigen, denn sie werden Barmherzigkeit erlangen." This is the fifth Beatitude from the Gospel of Matthew (5:7).</note> specifically on that day when they shall hear the voice: “Come, you blessed of my Father, inherit the kingdom prepared for you from the beginning of the world because of your mercy.” <note>original: "Kommt ihr Gesegneten meines Vaters, besitzet das Reich..." This refers to the Last Judgment in Matthew 25:34, where salvation is granted to those who performed acts of mercy.</note>

## 20. On Zeal and Diligence.

From this <term>charity <note>original: "Mildtätigkeit." As established on the previous page, this refers to the active practice of mercy and generosity toward others.</note></term> springs supernatural zeal and diligence toward all virtues and toward every noble adornment. No one can feel this zeal except for a striving, charitable person. This is an internal, impetuous drive toward every virtue and toward <term>conformity with Christ <note>original: "Gleichförmigkeit mit Christus." The mystical goal of aligning one's life and character perfectly with the example of Jesus.</note></term> and his saints. In this zeal, a person desires to bring heart and mind, soul and body, and everything they are, everything they have, and everything they can attain, into harmony with the honor and praise of God. **D** This zeal commands a person to keep watch with reason and wise insight, and to practice the virtues with body and soul in righteousness. Through this supernatural zeal, all the powers of the soul are opened to God and made ready for all virtues. The conscience is made joyful and the grace of God is increased; virtue is practiced with delight and joy, and external works are transfigured. **D** Whoever has received this living zeal from God has expelled the <term>fifth deadly sin <note>In the traditional medieval list of seven capital vices, the fifth is "Acedia" or spiritual sloth.</note></term>, namely <term>spiritual sloth <note>original: "Trägheit des Geistes." This refers to a state of spiritual apathy, listlessness, or "soul-weariness" regarding divine things.</note></term> and a weariness of the virtues necessary for salvation. And at times, this living zeal even drives out the heaviness and sluggishness of our physical nature. Concerning this, Christ says:

> “Blessed are those who hunger and thirst for
righteousness, for they shall be satisfied,”

namely when the glory of God will reveal itself and fill everyone according to the measure of their love and righteousness.

### 21. On Moderation and Sobriety.

From this zeal arises <term>moderation and sobriety <note>original: "Mäßigkeit und Nüchternheit." In this context, these virtues refer to a "right measuring" of one's life, encompassing both physical temperance and an intellectual humility that does not overstep human limits.</note></term>, both inwardly and outwardly; for no one can truly maintain the right measure in sobriety unless they are very zealous and earnestly intent on preserving soul and body in righteousness. Sobriety separates the <term>higher powers of the soul <note>original: "höheren Seelen-Kräfte." According to medieval psychology, these are the rational and spiritual faculties (intellect, will, memory) which should remain distinct from the "sensory" or animal instincts.</note></term> from the sensory powers and guards a person against immoderation and excess. Sobriety wishes neither to taste nor to become acquainted with what is forbidden.

**D** The incomprehensible, exalted nature of God towers above all creatures in heaven and on earth; for everything that a creature perceives is itself a creature. But God is above all creatures, outside of creatures, and within creatures, and all created knowledge is too narrow to grasp Him. If, however, the creature is to grasp and understand God, it must be enraptured beyond itself into God and grasp God through God. Whoever then wished to know <term>what God is <note>A central theme in mystical theology: we can know *that* God exists, but the human mind cannot define *what* God’s essence is. Attempting to do so is seen here as a dangerous lack of intellectual sobriety.</note></term>, and sought to investigate it, would be doing something forbidden; they would lose their senses. See, thus all created light fails when it comes to recognizing what God is. The <term>"whatness" <note>original: "Washeit." A translation of the Latin "quidditas," referring to the fundamental essence or nature of a thing.</note></term> of God eludes all creatures; but *that* He is, this is testified by nature, the Scripture, and all creatures. The articles of faith must be believed and not "known" through intellectual proof, for that is impossible as long as we are here; this is moderation. The hidden, profound teaching of the Scripture, which the Holy Spirit has inspired, should be explained in no other way than according to the life of Christ and his saints, and

<summary>This page defines the virtues of moderation and sobriety, applying them not only to physical behavior but to intellectual humility regarding the nature of God. The author argues that God's essence is beyond human comprehension and must be approached through faith rather than rational investigation.</summary>

Man should also show propriety and measure in his words and works, in silence, in speaking, in eating and drinking, in his actions and omissions according to the **custom of the Church** and the example of the saints.

## 22. Concerning Purity.

From this moderation arises the **purity of the soul and the body**, for no one can be perfectly pure in body and soul except the one who is moderate in body and soul. The **purity of the spirit** consists in this: that a person binds themselves to no creature with <term>sensory affection <note>original: "sinnlicher Zuneigung." This refers to an emotional or physical attachment to worldly things or people that distracts the soul from its focus on the divine.</note></term>, but clings to God alone; for one should make use of all creatures, but enjoy only God. 

Purity of the spirit binds a person to God beyond all understanding and feeling, and beyond all the gifts that God can pour into the soul. For whatever the creature receives in the intellect or in the senses: purity desires to leave all that behind and rest in God. One should not approach the <term>Sacrament of the Altar <note>A traditional term for the Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note></term> for the sake of "tasting" it, nor out of desire, nor for spiritual joy, peace, reassurance, or sweetness, nor for any other thing, but solely for the glory of God and to grow in all virtues. This

It defines spiritual purity as the total detachment from created things to find rest in God alone, even during religious acts like Communion.</summary>

...is purity of the spirit. 

The **purity of the heart** consists in this: that in the face of every sensory temptation or natural impulse, a person voluntarily turns to God with ever-renewed trust, without wavering, with constant loyalty, and with a resolute will to remain always with God. For consenting to sin or to the lust that the physical nature craves like an animal—that is separation from God. 

The **purity of the body** consists in this: that a person flees and avoids unchaste works, of whatever kind they may be, as soon as their conscience bears witness and warns them that they are unchaste and contrary to the commandments, the honor, and the will of God. 

Through these three kinds of purity, the **seventh deadly sin**, which is **unchastity** <note>original: "Unkeuschheit." In historical theology, this is the last of the seven deadly sins, commonly referred to as Lust.</note>, is driven away. It is a turning away of the spirit from God toward a created thing, accompanied by lust; an unchaste work of the body contrary to the permission of the Holy Church; and a carnal devotion of the heart to the tasting and enjoying of any creature. By this, I do not mean those sudden movements of desire or longing that no one can prevent <note>The author distinguishes between involuntary impulses—which were seen as a natural part of the human condition—and the "consent of the will," which constitutes the actual sin.</note>. 

Now you must know that **purity of the spirit** keeps a person in the **likeness of God**, far from all anxiety concerning created things, turned toward God and united with Him.

### 23. Of three enemies that are to be overcome through justice.

If we now wish to possess these virtues and drive out their opposites [the vices], we must possess **justice** <note>original: "Gerechtigkeit." In a medieval or early modern theological context, this often refers to "Righteousness"—the state of being right with God and acting according to His law.</note> and must practice and preserve it until our death in purity of heart. For we have three powerful adversaries <note>These are traditionally understood in Christian teaching as the World, the Flesh, and the Devil.</note> who constantly, in every place and in many ways, tempt and assault us. If we make peace with even one of these and follow him, then we will be defeated, for they are united in all injustice.

## 24. Concerning the Kingdom of the Soul.

Whoever wishes to attain and preserve these virtues must adorn, order, and govern their soul like a kingdom. **Free will** is the king of the soul. He is free by nature and even freer through grace. He shall be crowned with a crown called **divine love**. The crown and the kingdom should be received from the Emperor, who is Lord and Master and King of Kings, and one should possess, rule, and keep them permanently.

And this king, the free will, shall dwell in the capital of the kingdom, namely in the **"desiring power"** <term>desiring power: original "begehrende Kraft," the faculty of the soul that seeks after and longs for what it perceives as good; in medieval psychology, this is where the will resides</term> of the soul. And he shall be adorned and clothed in a two-part garment. The right side of the garment is formed by a divine virtue called **Fortitude** <note>original: "Stärke," which can mean strength, but in this context refers to the spiritual fortitude necessary to pursue God despite obstacles.</note>, so that the king may be strong and mighty to overcome all obstacles and journey into heaven—the palace of the supreme Emperor—and bow his crowned head in love before the highest King in a longing to surrender himself, which is the truest work of love. Through this, one receives the crown, and with this, one adorns the crown and maintains the kingdom, to possess it for eternity.

The left side of the clothing, however, is formed by a fundamental virtue called **"Moral Strength"** <term>Moral Strength: original "Sittliche Kraft," referring to the internal discipline and ethical character required to govern one's behavior</term>. Through this, free will—this king—shall suppress all vices and fulfill all virtues and [govern] his kingdom—

<summary>This page introduces the metaphor of the soul as a kingdom governed by free will, which must be crowned with divine love and clothed in fortitude and moral strength. It explains that the will resides in the soul's "desiring power," from which it directs the person toward God and virtuous living.</summary>

These shall live alongside the king in a palace called the **"Rational Power"** <term>Rational Power: original "Vernünftige Kraft," the faculty of the soul responsible for reason and understanding.</term> of the soul. And they shall be clothed and adorned with a moral virtue called **Temperance**, so that the king may always proceed with deliberation in all his actions and omissions. 

Through the virtue of Knowledge, one shall cleanse the conscience of all defects and adorn it with all virtues; and through the virtue of Discernment, one should give and take, do and refrain, be silent and speak, fast and eat, hear and answer, and perform everything with insight and discernment, clad in a moral virtue called Moderation or Temperance. 

This king, the **Free Will**, shall also appoint a judge in his kingdom: **Justice**. This is a divine virtue when it springs from love, and it is one of the highest moral virtues. This judge shall dwell in the mind, in the midst of the kingdom—that is, in the **"Irascible Power"** <term>Irascible Power: original "Widerstrebende Kraft," the faculty of the soul that resists evil and strives for difficult goods; traditionally where the virtue of courage or righteous anger resides.</term>. And he shall be adorned with a moral virtue called **Prudence**, for Justice cannot be perfect without Prudence. This judge, Justice, shall travel through the land, equipped with the power and authority of the king and enlightened by the insight of his advisors and his own prudence. And he shall appoint and depose, judge...

D And the common people of the kingdom—that is, all the **powers of the soul** <term>Powers of the soul: The various faculties such as memory, understanding, and will that animate the human person.</term>—they shall be grounded in humility and the fear of God, and be subject to God in all virtues, each power according to its own nature. ### 25. Concerning a spiritual encounter between God and us.

When a person has become seeing through the **grace of God**, when he has a pure conscience, has taken to heart the threefold coming of Christ, our Bridegroom, and has "gone out" in virtues, then the encounter with the Bridegroom occurs; and that is the fourth and final point. In this encounter lies all our **bliss** <note>original: "Seligkeit," meaning both spiritual happiness and eternal salvation.</note>, and in it the beginning and end of all virtues are enclosed; without this encounter, no virtue is truly practiced. D If anyone wishes to encounter Christ as his beloved Bridegroom and possess eternal life in him and with him, then he must encounter Christ here in time in three ways. The first is that he aims for God in all things through which one earns eternal life. The second is that he sets nothing alongside God that he [values] more than God or equal to God...

# Here begins the second book of the
# Ornament of the Spiritual Marriage.

**Preface.** The wise virgin—that is, the pure soul who has renounced earthly things and lives for God in virtue—has taken the oil of love and divine works into the vessel of her heart, along with the lamp of an unsullied conscience. But when Christ, the **Bridegroom** <term>Bridegroom: (*Bräutigam*) In mystical tradition, this refers to Christ as the spouse of the human soul, a metaphor drawn from the Song of Songs and the Parable of the Ten Virgins.</term>, withholds His comfort and the renewed outpouring of His gifts, then the soul becomes sleepy; she slumbers and grows sluggish <note>The author refers to the spiritual "dryness" or "darkness" where the soul feels distant from God's presence.</note>. In the middle of the night, when one least expects or suspects it, a spiritual cry resounds within the soul: “See, the bridegroom comes; go out to meet him.” 

Concerning this seeing, and the inner coming of Christ, and the spiritual going out of man to meet Christ, we wish to speak and to explain and interpret these four points of an inward, God-desiring practice <note>original: *gottverlangenden Übung*</note>, which many people attain through moral virtue and inner fire. ⸿ Through those words, Christ teaches us four things. First, He desires that our understanding be enlightened with supernatural light. We grasp this from that word which He speaks: “See.” Second, He shows us,

### 1. How one becomes supernaturally seeing in the “Inward Exercise.”

Now then, concerning the first point. Christ says: “See.” If a person is to see supernaturally in the **Inward Exercise** <term>Inward Exercise: (*Innige Übung*) The practice of contemplative prayer and internal focus, moving beyond mere outward religious actions.</term>, three things are necessarily required. 

The first is the light of divine **grace**, and indeed in a higher degree than one can feel it in the external, active life, without inner fire. 

The second is the rejection of images foreign [to God] and the emptiness of the heart, so that the person is free and image-less <note>The author promotes a "path of negation" (apophatic theology), suggesting that to see God, one must clear the mind of all worldly mental pictures and distractions.</note> and detached from all creatures and free of them. 

The third is a free turning of the will with a gathering of all powers, both bodily and spiritual, unburdened by any disordered love, flowing into the unity of God and into the unity of the spirit, so that the rational creature may supernaturally become a partaker of the high unity of God and possess it. For this reason, God created heaven and earth and all things, and for this reason He became man and taught us and lived for us and has

## 2. Concerning the threefold unity which is in us by nature.¹

Now pay close attention! One finds a threefold unity naturally in all people, and likewise supernaturally in good people. 

The first and highest unity of man is in God; for all creatures depend with their essence <term>Essence: (*Wesenheit*) The fundamental nature or "being" of a thing that makes it what it is.</term>, their life, and their preservation upon this unity. If they were separated from God in this regard, they would fall into nothingness and become naught. This unity is natural to our essence, whether we are good or evil. And without our cooperation, it makes us neither holy nor blessed. We possess this unity within ourselves and yet above us, as the starting point and the preservation of our being and our life. 

A second union or unity is likewise in us by nature. This is the unity of the higher powers <note>In medieval faculty psychology, the "higher powers" of the soul refer to the memory, the intellect, and the will.</note>, insofar as they actively take their natural origin in the **unity of the spirit**² <note>original: *Einheit des Geistes*</note>. This is the same unity as that which depends on God, but here it is understood in its activity, whereas in the first instance it was understood as essence. However, the spirit in both unities is ful— <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence; likely "vollkommen" (completely or perfectly).</note>

¶ The third unity, which is in us by nature, is the seat of the bodily powers in the unity of the heart, the beginning and origin of physical life. These three unities exist in man by nature as one life and one kingdom. In the lowest unity we are sensory <note>original: *sensibilis*</note> and animalistic; in the middle one we are rational and spiritual; in the highest one we are preserved according to our essence. And so it is by nature in all people.

### 3. Concerning the Inflow of Divine Grace into Our Spirit.

From this unity, in which the spirit is immediately united with God, grace flows and all gifts stream forth. And from this very unity, where the spirit rests in God above itself, Christ, the eternal Truth, speaks: "Behold, the bridegroom comes, go out to meet him." <note>Matthew 25:6. This scripture serves as the central theme for the "Inward Life" in the works of Jan van Ruusbroec.</note> Christ, who is the light of eternal truth, says: "Behold," for through him we become seeing. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Inward Life," specifically detailing how divine grace originates in the "unity of the spirit" and flows outward into the virtues before returning to its source.</meta>

...[to] flow out into all virtues, and into which they return again in the bond of love. » In this unity lies the power, the beginning and the end of all natural and supernatural activity of the creature, insofar as it is performed in a creaturely way with the help of grace, divine gifts, and by its own power. And God therefore pours His grace into the unity of the **highest powers** <term>Highest powers: (*obersten Kräfte*) In medieval psychology, these are the spiritual faculties of the soul—memory, intellect, and will—at the point where they converge in the spirit's essence.</term>, so that through the power, the richness, and the impulse of grace, man may practice virtue at all times; for God gives grace for active work, and Himself, above all graces, for enjoyment and for rest.

The unity of our spirit is our dwelling place in divine peace and in the richness of love; and all the diversity of virtues gathers there and lives there in the simplicity of the spirit. Now, divine grace, which flows from God, is an inward driving and urging <note>original: "Treiben und Jagen." This suggests a dynamic, restless movement or "chase" initiated by the Holy Spirit to stir the soul into action.</note> of the Holy Spirit, who prompts our spirit from within and kindles it toward all virtues. This grace flows from within, not from without, for God is more inward to us than we are to ourselves <note>This reflects the classic mystical concept, famously articulated by St. Augustine, that God is *interior intimo meo*—more internal than my innermost self.</note>, and His inward urging or working within us—whether natural or supernatural—is closer and more intimate to us than our own acting. And therefore, God works in us from the inside out, whereas all creatures act upon us from the outside in. And therefore, grace and all divine gifts and the "inward speaking" of God come from within, in the unity of our spirit, not from without, in the imagination with sensory images.

<summary>Ruusbroec explains that the "unity of the spirit" is the focal point where God dwells and where grace enters the soul. He emphasizes that divine action is unique because it moves from the inside out, unlike external sensory influences.</summary>

## 4. How we should base "Inward Exercise" upon imageless freedom.

Now, Christ speaks spiritually within the person who has turned toward God: "Behold." Three things, as I have said, make a person see within <term>Inward Exercise: (*Innig Übung*) A focused spiritual practice or discipline of the soul directed toward internal communion with God.</term>. The first is the shining-in <note>original: *Einleuchten*. This describes an internal illumination where divine light becomes visible to the "eye" of the soul.</note> of the grace of God. The grace of God in the soul is like a candle in a lantern or in a glass vessel; for it illuminates, transfigures, and shines through the vessel—that is, the good person. And grace reveals itself to the person who carries it within, so that they might be inwardly intent upon the observation of themselves. And through them, through their virtues and their good example, it is also made manifest to other people. The irradiation of God's grace seizes and moves the person inwardly from within, as fast as lightning; and this swift movement is the first thing that makes us see. 

From this rapid movement of God, the second thing arises through the cooperation of the human being: namely, the gathering of all inner and outer powers into the unity of the spirit in the bond of love. The third is freedom, so that the person may—without images <note>original: *bilderlos*. In mystical theology, "imagelessness" is a state where the mind is cleared of sensory distractions, mental pictures, and worldly preoccupations to better perceive God.</note> and without hindrance—keep an inward turning <note>original: *Einkehr*. A "recollection" or retreat into one's own soul.</note> into themselves as often as they wish, and as often as they remember their God. This means that the person is indifferent to what is pleasant or sad, to gain or loss, to elevation or humiliation, to the worries of others, to joy and to fear, and is captured by no created thing. These three things make a person see

<summary>This page outlines the three components of spiritual "sight": the illumination of divine grace, the unification of the soul's faculties through love, and the achievement of "imageless freedom" or detachment from worldly concerns.</summary>

### 5. On the threefold coming of our Lord in the inward person.

Even if the eyes were clear and the sight sharp, without a lovely <note>original: *minnelichen*. This refers to *Minne*, a Middle High German term for a specialized, mystical, and courtly love between the soul and God.</note>, attractive object, sharp vision would be of little, or even no use or benefit. And therefore Christ shows to the enlightened eyes of the understanding what we ought to see, namely the inward coming of Christ, our Bridegroom.

¶ Three kinds of special comings of God can be observed in those who practice the "Inward Life" with devotion; and through each of these three comings, the person is raised to a higher stage of life and to a more inward exercise.

¶ The first coming of Christ in "Inward Exercise" drives and hunts the person palpably from within, and pulls the person with all their powers upward toward heaven, and summons them to unity with God. This driving and pulling is felt in the heart and in the unity of all physical powers, but especially in the "Desiring Power" <term>Desiring Power (*Begehrende Kraft*): In medieval psychology, this is the faculty of the soul (the *appetitus*) responsible for longing, yearning, and emotional drive.</term>. For this coming drives and works in the lower part of the soul <note>The "lower part" usually refers to the senses and emotions, which must be purified before the "higher part" (reason and will) can fully unite with the divine.</note> of the person, for that part must be completely purified and adorned, inflamed and drawn inward. ### 6. On the second arrival of our Lord in the inward person.
The second kind of inward arrival of Christ, in greater nobility and likeness to Himself and combined with even more gifts and enlightenments, is an inflowing <term>Inflowing (*Einströmen*): A common mystical metaphor describing God's grace or essence pouring into the soul like water into a vessel.</term> into the highest power of the soul with the riches of divine gifts, which strengthen, enlighten, and enrich the spirit in manifold ways. This inflowing of God into us demands from us an outflowing and a back-flowing <note>original: *Zurückfluten*. This describes the mystical movement of the soul returning its gifts and its very self to the Divine Source, completing a spiritual circle.</note> of all that wealth into the same ground from which the flow originated. In this inflowing, God grants and reveals great wonders; but He demands back from the soul all His gifts, far beyond everything that the creature is capable of achieving. This exercise and this state is nobler and more God-like than the first; and by this, the three highest powers of the soul <note>In medieval theology, these are typically the Memory, Understanding, and Will.</note> are adorned.
