## 10. How the Virtues Arise from This Unity.

From this unity arises <term>inwardness</term> <note>original: "Innigkeit." In this mystical tradition, it refers to a state of being "gathered" or focused entirely within the soul's center to meet God.</note>, for no one can be inward unless they are gathered in unity within themselves. <term>Inwardness</term> means that a person has turned inward from the outside into their own heart, so that they can understand and feel the influence or the "in-speaking" of God. <term>Inwardness</term> is a sensible fire of love which the Spirit of God has ignited, and which stirs the person from within; and they do not know from whence it comes, or what has happened to them.

## 11. Of Sensible, Feelable Love.

From <term>inwardness</term> there arises a sensible love that permeates the human heart and the "desiring power" <note>The "desiring power" (begehrende Kraft) refers to the faculty of the soul that moves it toward what it perceives as good.</note> of the soul. No one can possess this longing love, combined with the sensible delight of the heart, unless they are of an inward mind. Sensible <term>divine love</term> <note>original: "Minne."</note> and love is a longing, blessed desire that one cherishes toward God as toward an eternal Good, in which every good is contained. Sensible love takes leave of all creatures regarding joy <note>This means the soul no longer finds its primary emotional satisfaction in created things, though it still uses them for survival.</note>, though not insofar as they are necessary. Inward love feels itself touched from within by the eternal <term>divine love</term>, to which it must ever dedicate itself. Inward love easily leaves and scorns all things so that it may attain what it <term>loves</term> <note>original: "minnt," the verb form of "Minne."</note>.

## 12. Of Fervor.

From this sensible love arises a fervent longing for God and His honor, for no one can carry a fervent longing in their heart unless they have sensible love and <term>divine love</term> <note>original: "Minne." As noted previously, this refers to the intense, reciprocal love between the soul and God.</note> for God. When the fire of <term>divine love</term> sends its yearning flame upward toward heaven, that is fervor. Fervor drives and fires a person from without and from within toward the service of God. Fervor causes body and soul to blossom in honor and reverence for God and all people. God demands fervor from us in every service we are meant to perform for Him. Fervor purifies body and soul from all things that could hold us back or hinder us. Fervor shows and grants the right path to salvation.

## 13. Of Gratitude.

Gratitude arises from glowing fervor; for no one can thank and praise God as well as the inward, fervent person. With good reason we should thank and praise God, since He created us as rational creatures and appointed and established heaven and earth and the angels for our service; that He became human for the sake of our sins, and that He taught us, lived for us, and guided us. And that He served us in humble garments and suffered a shameful death for us, and promised His eternal kingdom and Himself for our reward and exaltation. And that He spares us in our sins and, moreover, altogether <note>The text breaks off here and continues on the following page.</note>

### 14. Concerning the Twofold Woe that Arises from Inward Gratitude

Out of inward gratitude and loving praise grow two kinds of woe: pain of the heart and of longing. 

The first woe is that one falls short in thanks, in praise, in honor, and in the service of God; the second is that one does not increase, as one would wish, in love, in the virtues, in faithfulness, and in perfect conduct, in order to become worthy to thank, praise, and serve God as is His due. This is the second woe. Both are root and fruit, beginning and end of all inward virtue. 

O pain and woe of the soul regarding the falling behind in the life of virtue and the praise of God: this is the highest work of this first way of inward practice, and with this, one reaches its perfection.

## 17. Of the second way, which strengthens inwardness through humility.

Since we now compare these four ways of Christ’s coming with the light and the power of the sun, we observe another such power and effect of the sun, by which fruit ripens more quickly and multiplies. When the sun rises higher and higher and enters the sign of <term>Gemini</term> <note>Original: *Zwillinge*. The "Twins" of the zodiac. Medieval writers often used the zodiac to explain the seasons of the soul; here, Gemini represents the doubling of spiritual energy in late spring.</note> [this is a twofold thing of the same nature], which takes place in mid-May, the sun exerts double power upon the flowers and herbs and upon everything that is growing on earth. If the planets that govern nature are then favorably positioned, as the season requires, the sun casts its rays upon the earth and draws moisture up into the air. From this come dew and rain, and the fruit thrives and multiplies greatly.

𝔇 Something similar takes place when Christ, the bright sun, stands high above all things in our hearts, and the demands of the bodily nature, which conflict with the spirit, are well-restrained and properly ordered according to wise insight; when the virtues have become a spiritual possession in the manner you heard in the first way, and when through the heat of love all the pleasure and rest that one tastes in the virtues [is offered up] with thanks and praise...

...insight, then virtues and good works are offered and presented to God. Then, at times, there falls a sweet rain of new inward consolation and the heavenly dew of divine sweetness. Through this, all virtues grow and double themselves, provided that everything proceeds in an orderly fashion. This is a special, new influence and a new arrival of Christ into the <term>loving heart</term> <note>Original: *minnende Herz*. This refers to the tradition of *Minne*, or mystical love, where the soul seeks a direct, passionate union with the Divine.</note>. And by this means, a person is placed into a higher state than they possessed before. At this peak, Christ says:

> “Go out according to the manner of this coming.”

### 18. Of the chaste pleasure of the heart and the sensory powers.

From this sweetness arises a <term>delight</term> <note>Original: *Wollust*. While this word often means "lust" in modern German, in medieval mystical texts it describes a holy, intense, and chaste pleasure or "spiritual delectation" that affects both soul and body.</note> of the heart and all bodily powers, so that it seems to the person that they are being embraced from within by God in a loving hold. This delight and this consolation are—both spiritually and physically—greater and more joyful than all the bliss the earth can offer, even if a person were to taste it in its entirety. In this delight, God lowers Himself into the heart by means of His gifts with so much tangible consolation and joy that the heart overflows within. This allows the person to recognize how miserable those are who live outside of love. This delight is an overflowing of the heart, such that the person is unable to restrain themselves because of the fullness of the inward joy.

From this bliss arises <term>spiritual drunkenness</term> <note>Original: *geistliche Trunkenheit*. A state of "sober intoxication" where the soul is so filled with divine grace that it transcends ordinary reason and sensory control.</note>. This occurs when

### 20. What can harm a person in this intoxication.

## 21. A parable of how a person should practice and conduct themselves at this stage.

I wish to tell you a short **parable** <note>Original: *Gleichnis*. Ruusbroec frequently uses natural allegories to explain complex psychological and spiritual states.</note> so that you do not go astray, but rather govern yourselves wisely in this **state of the soul**. You should take to heart the example of the **wise bee** and act as she does: She dwells in **unity** in the midst of the assembly of her community and flies out (not in the storm, but in still, calm weather and sunshine) to all the flowers where sweetness is to be found. She rests on no flower and does not linger over its beauty and sweetness; instead, she draws **honey and wax** from it—that is, *sweetness* and *light-matter* <note>Original: *Leuchtstoff*. This refers to the wax used to make candles, symbolizing the light of understanding or truth.</note>—and brings it into the **unity** formed by all the bees, so that her labor may bring fruit and great benefit. 

When **Christ**, the eternal **Sun**, shines into the heart opened to Him, He causes the heart and its inner powers to sprout, bloom, and grow through joys and sweetness. The wise person will act like the bees; they will fly with deliberation, with **reason** and **discernment** <note>Original: *Unterscheidung*. This refers to "discretion," the ability to distinguish between genuine divine influence and mere emotionalism or deception.</note>, to all the **gifts** and all the **sweetness** they have ever tasted, and to every good thing that God has ever shown them. With the **tongue of love** and inward consideration, they will savor the full abundance of **comfort** and goodness, yet they will not stay on any flower of...

## 22. Of the third mode of the spiritual coming of Christ.

When the sun in the sky reaches its highest point and enters the sign of <term>Cancer</term> <note>original: *Cancer* (Latin for "The Crab"). This refers to the summer solstice, the peak of the sun's power before it begins its symbolic "backward" descent.</note>, so that it can climb no higher but begins to move backward, the heat is greatest in all the year. The sun draws the moisture upward, the soil becomes completely dry, and the fruit now ripens best. 

Something similar happens when Christ, the divine sun, has ascended in our heart as if to His zenith—that is, above all gifts, consolations, and <term>sweetnesses</term> <note>original: *Süßigkeiten*. In the language of medieval mysticism, this refers to the pleasant emotional or sensory feelings experienced during prayer or meditation.</note> that we can receive from Him. At this stage, we do not remain resting in any joy that God may pour into our soul, no matter how great it may be. 

If we have mastery over ourselves and return inward again and again, as shown previously, with humble praise and heartfelt thanks into that same <term>ground</term> <note>original: *Grund*. A technical term in Rhineland mysticism referring to the deepest, most essential part of the soul where the human spirit and the Divine meet.</note> from which all gifts flow according to the need and merit of the creature: then Christ has ascended to the highest point and stands, as it were, at the zenith of our heart, desiring to draw all things—that is, all our powers—into Himself. When no joy or consolation can overcome or hold back the <term>loving heart</term> <note>original: *minnende Herz*. The word *minne* refers to a specific medieval concept of ecstatic, selfless, and noble love between the soul and God.</note>...

## 23. Concerning the Torment and Restlessness of Love.
This internal summoning and urging, as well as the fact that the creature soars upward and offers itself willingly with all that it can achieve, without being able to reach or attain that unity: this causes a spiritual torment. When the heart’s innermost part and the origin of life are wounded by love, and one cannot attain what one desires above all else, and when one must always remain where one does not wish to be, then from these two things arises that torment. 

Here Christ has, as it were, ascended to the zenith <note>original: *Scheitelpunkt*. Literally the "summit" or "crown," referring to the highest point of the human spirit where it meets the divine.</note> of the <term>Gemüt</term> <note>original: *Gemütes*. A difficult term to translate, meaning the seat of one's deepest emotions, mind, and soul combined.</note> and casts his divine rays into the heart’s hungry desire and longing, and this fiery <term>irradiation</term> <note>original: *Einstrahlung*. A technical term for the direct influence of divine light upon the soul's faculties.</note> dries up and consumes all moisture—that is, all the strength and power of nature <note>In medieval spiritual thought, "nature" refers to our human instincts and self-will, which must be "dried up" or refined by divine fire to allow for grace.</note>. 

The greedy, gaping heart and the shining in of the divine rays produce a constant torment. » When one then can neither attain God, nor wishes to nor can do without Him, then from this conflict within the person arises a thirst for love and a restlessness from without and within. At the time when a person is so maddened by love <note>original: *liebewütig*. A powerful term describing a state of holy frenzy or desperate, passionate longing for God that overwhelms the senses.</note>, no [one]...

Deeply felt madness of love consumes the human heart and drinks its blood. Here, the sensible <term>glow of love</term> <note>original: *Liebesglut*. A traditional metaphor suggesting that the physical body is a cell that prevents the soul from achieving total union with God.</note>, so that they may be united with the one they love.

Historically, it was feared for causing extreme heat, disease, and mental instability.</note> begin, which bring many plagues with them. And the sun is not solely to blame for this, for if it were, conditions would be the same in all lands, in all waters, and among all people; rather, the cause often lies in the poor quality and lack of balance in the matter influenced by the sun. 

Something similar occurs when a person enters that state of restlessness. He actually falls into the **dog days** <note>The "dog days" (*Hundstage*) refer to the hottest period of summer. In mysticism, this represents a period of intense spiritual "heat" or trial.</note>, and the glow of the divine rays burns so fiercely and so hot from above, and the loving, wounded heart is so ignited from within (when the heat of love and the impatience of desire catch fire in such a way), that the person falls into restlessness and remains unsatisfied, just like a woman who is in labor and cannot give birth. If the person then wishes to look constantly into their own wounded heart and upon the one they love, the pain constantly increases. 

And the torment grows until the physical nature of the person withers and dries up, just like the trees in a hot land, and he finally dies of **love-madness** <note>original: *Liebeswut*. A state of ecstatic, overwhelming longing for God.</note> and ascends to heaven without **purgatory** <note>The author suggests that the intense suffering of divine love on earth serves as a complete purification, making the afterlife state of cleansing unnecessary.</note>. Truly, he who dies of love dies a blessed death; however, as long as the tree can still bear good fruit, one should not cut it down or uproot it. At times, **God** flows with great sweetness into the heart maddened by love; then the heart swims in **bliss**,

### 27. A Parable of the Ant.

I wish to give a short parable to those who walk in **love-frenzy** <note>original: *Liebeswut*. An ecstatic state of overwhelming, sometimes physically exhausting, longing for the Divine.</note>, so that they may pass through this state worthily and properly and attain higher virtues. There is a little "creature" <note>original: *Würmchen*. Literally "little worm," a term historically used for any small crawling insect.</note> called an ant. This ant is strong and clever and has a resilient life. And she loves to live in the **unity** of her community in warm, dry soil. And in the summer she works and gathers food and grain for the winter. And she splits the grain in two, so that it does not rot or spoil, but may instead be used when one can no longer gather anything. And she does not take strange paths, but always follows one path. And when she waits for the right time, she is able to fly.

So should those people also behave. They should be strong in the expectation of the **coming of Christ** and be wise regarding the deceptions and suggestions of the Evil Enemy. They should not desire to die <note>A common theme in mysticism where the soul, in its intense longing for God, wishes for physical death to achieve union.</note>, but always prepare praise for God and acquire new virtues for themselves. They should dwell in the **unity of their heart** and their powers and obey the **summons and urging** <note>original: *Heischen und Drängen*. This refers to the inner "pull" or demand felt by the soul to return to its source in God.</note> of the divine Unity. They should dwell in warm, dry soil, that is, in strong...

### 30. A parable of how one may be hindered in this fourth stage.
At this season, when day and night are of equal length, the sun begins to sink and the weather becomes cooler. The result is that some careless people take in <term>bad humors</term> <note>original: "böse Säfte"; referring to the medieval medical theory of humorism, where an imbalance of bodily fluids was thought to cause physical and spiritual illness</note>. These humors fill the stomach, attack the health, and produce various diseases; they ruin the desire and the taste for any good food, and even bring death to some. Through bad humors, some people degenerate and contract <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: "Wassersucht"; a condition of swelling caused by excess fluid, often used by medieval writers as a metaphor for spiritual pride or an insatiable "thirst" for worldly things</note>, a disease from which they suffer for a long time and which brings death to many. Through superfluous humors, illnesses and fevers arise, from which many people suffer or even die. It is similar for all those people who are of good will or who even once **tasted God**, but who later fall and wander away from God and the truth. They wither away on the path of true spiritual progress, or they er...

However, the undiscerning person who lacks self-control easily falls into these diseases because they have grown colder within. Consequently, their nature slackens in the practice of virtues and good works. At times, they dishonestly—and more than is necessary—crave comfort and the sparing of the body. Some would gladly receive **divine consolation** if it could be granted to them without effort or labor. Others seek relief from **created things** <note>original: "Geschöpfen"; in mystical theology, this refers to anything that is not God, such as other people, worldly distractions, or physical pleasures</note>, from which great harm often arises. And some believe themselves to be weak, delicate, and entirely exhausted, imagining that they need everything they can get or every bit of rest and comfort they can grant their bodies. When a person turns toward physical needs and bodily comforts in this way, yielding to them excessively, these are all **bad humors** <note>original: "böse Säfte"; referring to the medieval medical theory that an imbalance of fluids causes sickness</note> that fill the stomach—that is, the human heart—and take away the taste and desire for good food, which is to say, for all the virtues.

...at one moment they want to lead a wandering life, and then suddenly shut themselves in a **cell** <note>original: "Klause"; a small, private room or hermitage for solitary prayer and asceticism.</note>. At times they want to go to **Communion** <note>original: "Kommunion"; the central Christian sacrament of the Eucharist.</note> often, and shortly thereafter they place little value on it. Sometimes they want to pray a great deal, and soon after, observe much silence. This is a hunt for novelty and **inconstancy** <note>original: "Unbeständigkeit"; a spiritual state of being fickle or easily swayed by changing emotions or whims.</note>, which holds back and prevents a person from understanding the inner truth; it deprives **inwardness** <term>inwardness (Innigkeit): a focused, contemplative state of spiritual depth and closeness to God</term> of its foundation and makes its practice impossible.

**D** Pay attention now to where this inconstancy in pious people comes from. If a person directs their focus and their inner striving more toward virtues and outward practices than toward God and unity with Him, then they do indeed remain in God's **grace** (for in their virtues they direct their intention toward God), yet their life is inconstant, for they do not feel themselves resting in God above all virtues. And so they possess Him and do not know it; for the One whom they seek in virtues and in numerous practices, they already carry within themselves—above good intentions, above virtues, and above all practices. Therefore, if a person is to overcome this inconstancy, they must learn to rest in God and in the high **unity of God** <note>original: "Einheit Gottes"; a mystical concept of the soul's direct union with the divine essence.</note> above all virtues.

**D** The other fever of inconstancy, which arises from **cold**, affects all those people whose good intention is indeed directed toward God, but who also seek and strive for other things alongside Him in a disordered way. This fever stems from coldness; for there is little **glow of love** <note>original: "Liebesglut"; the passionate, burning devotion to God that characterizes the highest spiritual state.</note> where foreign things, alongside God, are the occasion and driving force of virtuous works. These people have inconstant hear- <note>The text breaks off mid-word; likely "hearts" (Herzens).</note>

<vocab>wandering life, cell, Communion, inconstancy, inwardness, virtues, God, glow of love, driving force</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of spiritual "fevers," specifically focusing on the psychological and spiritual pitfalls of religious people who are driven by ego rather than genuine devotion. It completes the word "Herzens" (heart) from the previous page's final sentence about the "unity of God." The text uses the decorative letter 'D' to mark new sections or sub-points in the argument.</meta>

...heart, for in everything they do, nature secretly seeks its own interest <note>original: "das Ihrige"; referring to the "natural" ego or self-will that operates beneath the surface of seemingly pious actions.</note>, though often without them knowing it, for they do not truly know themselves. They choose and then reject first one way, then another. Today they want to confess their whole life to one person and seek advice, and tomorrow they choose another. They ask for advice regarding all things, but they rarely follow anyone’s counsel. Everything for which they are blamed or disapproved of, they would like to excuse and gloss over. They have many beautiful words, but there is little behind them. They often like to reap, through small works, the honor that is normally paid to virtue. They wish for their virtues to become known, which is why they are vain and find pleasure neither in God nor in themselves. They want to instruct other people, but they are very reluctant to accept instruction or criticism themselves. Natural self-love and hidden pride make them **inconstant**. Such people walk along the edge of hell; just one more small misstep and they fall in.

**D** From this fever of inconstancy, a **quartan fever** <note>original: "Viertagsfieber"; historically a type of malaria where the fever recurs every fourth day. In this spiritual context, it represents a recurring, cyclical state of alienation that is harder to break than a simple mood.</note> sometimes arises in some people; this is an **estrangement** <term>estrangement (Entfremdung): a state of spiritual "alienation" where a person feels like a stranger to God, to their own soul, and to the truth.</term> from God, from oneself, from truth, and from all virtue. And then the person falls into such confusion that he does not know what is wrong with him or what he should do. This sickness is more dangerous than any of the preceding ones.

**D** Through this estrangement, the person sometimes gets a fever that is called the double quartan fever—**indifference** <note>original: "Gleichgültigkeit"; a state of spiritual apathy or listlessness where the soul no longer cares for its progress or its relationship with the divine.</note>. Then it repeats itself on the fourth [day]...

<summary>This page describes how hidden pride and self-interest lead to spiritual inconstancy and eventually to "quartan fever"—a state of deep estrangement and confusion. It warns that such vanity and refusal to accept criticism bring a person to the brink of spiritual ruin.</summary>

### 33. How we find these four modes perfectly in Christ.

We must walk in the light so that we do not go astray, and we must look to Christ, who taught us these four modes and preceded us in their practice. Christ, the brightly shining sun, rose in the heaven of the high Trinity and in the dawn of his glorious mother, the Virgin Mary, who was and is the dawn and the daybreak of all graces, in which we may rejoice eternally.

He was thus the only-begotten Son of the Father and united with human nature. And he was inward, for he brought to earth the fire that all the saints...

When the appointed time had come for Christ to harvest and gather into the eternal kingdom all the fruits of virtue <note>original: "Tugend"; here meaning moral excellence and spiritual power.</note> that had ever been worked or would yet be worked in the future, then the eternal sun began to set; for Christ humbled himself and surrendered his physical life into the hands of his enemies. He bore this with humble patience and, in this state of abandonment, performed the most sublime works of love. And through this, he regained and bought back our eternal inheritance. 

For this reason, the lower part of his humanity has received an adornment <note>original: "Schmuck"; in medieval mysticism, this refers to the spiritual beauty or "jewels" earned through suffering and merit.</note>, for it was in this human nature that he bore this hardship because of our sins. And therefore he is called the Savior of the world and has been transfigured, glorified, and exalted, and sits at the right hand of his Father and reigns with power. And every creature in heaven, on earth, and in hell bows its knees before his exalted name for all eternity.

<meta>This page transitions from describing the suffering and sacrifice of Christ (the "third mode") to the practical application for the individual believer. It outlines the specific moral and spiritual conditions necessary for a person to receive enlightenment, linking the "Active Life" of virtue with the "Inward Life" of contemplation.</meta>

### 34. How a Person Must Live to be Enlightened.

Whoever lives in righteous obedience and moral virtues according to the commandments of God, and practices the inner virtues according to the manner and impulse of the Holy Spirit—who leads and guides us in all things according to justice—who does not seek his own gain in time or eternity, who can receive and endure darkness, internal difficulties, and all kinds of misery in holy patience with equanimity, thanks God for everything, and offers himself to God in humble detachment <term>Gelassenheit: a central concept in German mysticism meaning "letting-go," detachment from self-will, and a calm, surrendered state of being.</term>, he has received the first coming of Christ according to the manner of the "Inward Exercise" <note>original: "Innigen Übung"; this refers to focused spiritual disciplines intended to cultivate the soul's inner relationship with God.</note>. He has "gone out" <note>In this context, "going out" refers to the soul leaving behind its selfish desires to move toward the divine.</note> through an inward life and has adorned within himself the vitality of his heart and his physical, sensory unity with rich virtues and gifts.

When a person is now well-purified, calmed, and drawn inward in his lower soul-part <note>In medieval spiritual psychology, the "lower part" of the soul relates to the senses and emotions, while the "higher part" relates to the intellect and spirit.</note>, he can, when God deems the time has come and commands it, be inwardly enlightened. He could even be enlightened right at the beginning of his conversion <note>original: "Kehr"; literally a "turning" of the heart toward God.</note>, if he would surrender himself completely to the will of God and utterly deny his own self; for everything depends on this. Then, of course, he would subsequently have to follow the modes and paths that were previously shown for both the "Active Life" and the "Inward Life" <note>original: "Tätige" and "Innige Leben"; the Active Life involves outward works of charity and duty, while the Inward Life involves contemplation.</note>. But this would be easier for him than for another who must begin from the bottom, for he would have more light than other people.

<summary>This page describes the spiritual requirements for enlightenment, emphasizing that while it usually follows a long path of virtue and "Inward Exercise," it can be granted immediately to those who practice total self-denial.</summary>
<keywords>enlightenment, obedience, detachment, Gelassenheit, Inward Life, Active Life, self-denial, Holy Spirit, virtues</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the first coming of Christ and the moral preparation of the soul. ### 35. Of the Second Coming of Christ.

Now we wish to speak further of the second manner of Christ’s coming in the "Inward Exercise" <note>original: "Innigen Übung"; a term for focused spiritual contemplation and discipline where the soul turns away from external distractions to seek God within.</note>, through which a person is adorned, enlightened, and enriched according to his three highest powers of the soul. We wish to compare this coming to a living spring with three streams.

This spring, from which the streams flow, is the fullness of God's grace in the [encompassing] unity of our spirit. There, grace rests in its entire essential scope like a full spring; however, in its effect, it flows out like streams into every power of the soul according to its need. These streams are special inflows or influences of God into the highest [soul] powers, in which God works in various ways by means of grace.

### 36. How the First Stream of this Spring Adorns the Memory.

The first stream of grace that God allows to flow during this coming is a pure simplicity, which enlightens the spirit without distinction <note>To see "without distinction" means to experience a unified awareness of the Divine, looking past the "multiplicity" of different thoughts or physical objects.</note>. This stream takes its origin from the source in the unity of the spirit. It flows straight downward and floods all the powers of the soul—the highest as well as the lowest—and lifts them above all multiplicity and distraction <note>original: "Unledigkeit"; this refers to a state of being "un-leisurely" or busily occupied with many worldly worries that prevent spiritual focus.</note>. It brings about simplicity in the person and shows and grants him inner recollectedness <note>original: "Gesammeltsein"; a state of being "gathered together" or focused, rather than scattered by various thoughts.</note> in the unity of his spirit. Thus, the person is lifted up regarding his [higher] memory <note>In medieval psychology, "Memory" (Gedächtnis) was the power of the soul that held one’s sense of self and the awareness of God’s presence, rather than just remembering facts.</note> and freed from...

And in response, the person goes out and perceives and finds that they are ordered and established, flooded and held fast by the light poured into them within the unity of their spirit, or "Memory" <note>original: "Gedächte" / Latin: "mentis"; in this context, "Memory" refers to the deep ground of the soul where one's spiritual essence is stored, rather than just the recall of facts.</note>. They feel themselves drawn to this unity both naturally and supernaturally, while this unity itself—by means of God's gifts and a "simple intention" <note>original: "einfache Meinung"; a technical term for a soul's single-minded focus on God without selfish motives.</note>—will have an eternal, loving inclination toward that high unity where the Father and the Son are united in the bond of the Holy Spirit with all the saints. And with this, the first stream, which demands unity, is satisfied.

<sig>III</sig>

<gloss>[As a Person=]</gloss>The characteristic property of the Holy Spirit: incomprehensible love and generosity, mercy and condescension, infinite faithfulness and benevolence, incomprehensible greatness, overflowing richness and abyssal goodness—flowing blissfully through all heavenly spirits—a fiery flame that glows everything together into unity, a flowing source, rich in flavor according to each one’s desire, a preparing and introducing of all saints into their eternal bliss, an embracing and a mutual-penetration of Father and Son and all saints in a unity of enjoyment <note>original: "genießender Einheit"; refers to "fruition," the state of resting in the final enjoyment of God</note>. All this is recognized and seen undivided and without parts in the simple nature of the Godhead. And yet, in our estimation, these characteristics stand in a personal <gloss>[=independent]</gloss> manner in manifold distinction from one another. For in our view there is a great difference between power and goodness, generosity and truth: nevertheless, all this stands in unity and undivided in the high nature of the Godhead. The relationships, however, that produce the personal characteristics, remain in an eternal distinction. For the Father creates distinction: He begets His Son without ceasing, but he himself is not begotten. And the Son is begotten, but cannot beget, and so the Father has a Son in eternity and the Son a Father. And these are the reciprocal relationships of the Father to the Son and the Son to the Father. And the Father and the Son breathe forth <term>breathe forth: a translation of "geisten" (Latin: spirare), the theological term for how the Holy Spirit proceeds from the Father and the Son</term> a Spirit, which is the will and love of them both. And this Spirit does not beget and is not begotten;

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Trinity, specifically focusing on the procession of the Holy Spirit from the Father and the Son, and how the divine essence flows outward into all creation. And these three Persons are one God and one spiritual Being. And all attributes, along with the works flowing from them, are common to all the Persons, for they act in the power of a single nature.

38. **How one falls into wonder regarding God’s universal outpouring.**

This incomprehensible wealth, this majesty and generously flowing communicativeness <note>original: "Mitteilsamkeit"; the quality of God sharing His very nature and gifts with others</note> of the divine nature draws man into wonder. He especially admires the supremely exalted outpouring of God’s universal benevolence; for he gazes upon this incomprehensible Being as a shared enjoyment <note>original: "Genuß"; refers to the theological concept of "fruition," the blissful state of resting in the final possession of God</note> of God and all the saints. And he beholds the divine Persons as an all-encompassing outpouring and working in the kingdom of grace and glory, in nature and the supernatural, in all places and at all times, in saints and ordinary people, in heaven and on earth, in all creatures—whether they be rational <gloss>[such as humans]</gloss>, irrational <gloss>[such as animals]</gloss>, or material—according to the value, the need, and the receptivity of each. And he beholds how heaven and earth, sun and moon, the four elements <note>The four classical elements: earth, water, air, and fire</note> together with all creatures, as well as the course of the firmament <term>firmament: in medieval cosmology, the sphere of the stars that rotates around the earth</term>, are common to all. God is communicative with all His gifts, the angels are communicative, and the soul is communicative toward all [its] powers, the entire body, and all its members, and it is whole in every member; for the soul cannot be divided, except in the imagination.

<summary>This passage describes the Holy Spirit's eternal procession and how God’s nature is one of infinite generosity, sharing His essence and gifts across all levels of existence from the heavens to the human soul. The author emphasizes that God's grace is universal, reaching all creatures according to their capacity to receive it.</summary>
<keywords>Holy Spirit, Trinity, Divine Nature, Outpouring, Benevolence, Fruition, Elements, Firmament, Soul and Body, Communicativeness</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the discussion of God's "universal outpouring," where the soul wonders at how God’s nature is shared across all of creation. It moves from general contemplation of God’s majesty to the specific way grace acts upon the human will.</meta>

For the higher and lower powers—the spirit and the soul—are indeed different in our conception, but according to their nature, they are one and the same. In this same way, God gives Himself to each individual entirely and in a unique manner, yet He remains common to all creatures; for through Him all things exist, and in Him and upon Him hang heaven and earth and all of nature. 

When a person thus considers the wondrous wealth and the majesty of the divine nature, along with all the manifold gifts that God gives and offers to His creatures, then wonder awakens within him at such manifold wealth, such majesty, and the infinite faithfulness that God holds toward His creatures. From this arises an extraordinary inner joy of the spirit and a high confidence in God; and this inner joy encompasses and flows through all the powers of the soul and the very marrow of the spirit.

## 39. How the third stream strengthens the will in every perfection.

Out of this joy, and out of the fullness of grace and divine faithfulness, the third stream <note>In this work, Ruysbroeck describes three "streams" of grace that flow from the divine unity into the soul to transform its different faculties (the intellect, the will, and the memory/vitality).</note> springs forth in that same unity of the spirit and flows out. This stream ignites the will like fire, and it swallows and consumes everything into unity. It overflows and permeates all the soul’s powers with rich gifts and a particular nobility, calling forth in the will a tender, spiritual love without labor <note>Ruysbroeck suggests that at this high stage of spiritual life, love becomes a natural, effortless response to God's presence rather than a difficult moral struggle.</note>. Now Christ speaks inwardly in the spirit by means of this stream of fire:

> “Go out with exercises according to the manner of this gift.”

<summary>Ruysbroeck explains that while we distinguish between the soul and spirit, they are one in nature, just as God is both uniquely present to the individual and common to all. He describes a "third stream" of grace that ignites the will with an effortless, spiritual love.</summary>

## 41. Concerning the first outgoing toward God and all the Saints.

Now understand, the person shall go out and contemplate God along with all the saints in His glory. He shall contemplate the rich, gentle outflowing of God—how He, according to the desire of all spirits, flows out into His saints with glory, with Himself, and with incomprehensible delight, and how they—with themselves and with everything they have received and are able to accomplish—flow back into that same rich unity from which all delight originates. This outflowing of God always demands a back-flowing <term>reflux: the mystical cycle where grace flows from God to the soul and the soul’s love flows back to God</term>, for God is a flowing and ebbing sea that flows without ceasing into all His beloved ones according to each one’s need and merit. And He allows all who have been enriched in heaven and on earth to flow back again with everything they possess and are capable of. 

From some, however, He demands more than they can accomplish. For He shows Himself so rich, so generous, and so boundlessly kind, and by behaving in this way, He demands a love and honor that are worthy of Him. For God wants to be loved by us according to His own nobility. But there all spirits succumb, and thus love becomes wayless <note>original: "weiselos"; this refers to a state of "modelessness" or "waylessness" where the soul’s love is so overwhelmed by God's infinity that it loses any specific method or human limit</note> and without boundaries; for they do not know how they should accomplish and carry it out. For the love of all spirits is limited; and therefore love starts again and again from...

## 42. Concerning the second outgoing toward all sinful people.

Sometimes, that person should descend with great compassion and gentle mercy toward sinners and lead them to God through fervor and much prayer. He should remind God of all the goodness that He is, all that He is capable of, and all that He has done and promised us—just as if God had forgotten it; for God desires to be petitioned. And love wishes to attain everything it desires, yet it seeks to be neither stubborn nor self-willed; instead, it entrusts everything to the rich goodness and generosity of God. For God loves without measure, and with this, the lover is gladly content. Since this person carries a universal love within themselves, they pray and desire that God pour out His love and mercy over heathens <note>original: "Heiden"; in this context, it refers to non-Christians or those outside the author's faith tradition</note>, over Jews, and over all unbelieving people, so that He may be loved, recognized, and praised in heaven, and so that our glory, joy, and peace may be increased at all the ends of the earth. This is the second outgoing—namely, toward the sinners.

At times, the person should also cast a gaze upon their friends in purgatory <note>original: "Fegfeuer"; a place of spiritual purification after death in medieval theology</note> and contemplate their misery, their longing, and their heavy torment. Then he should [bring] the favor, the mercy, and the generosity... <note>The text breaks off here and continues on the following page.</note>

### 45. How one can recognize those who are hindered in universal love.

One finds certain people who are very sharp-witted in words and skilled at proving high things, yet they experience nothing of these enlightened ways, nor of that generous, universal love <term>allgemeine Liebe</term> <note>original: "allgemeine Liebe"; a selfless, expansive love that extends to all creation without attachment to self</note>. So that such people may know themselves, and so others may also recognize them, I will make them known by three signs. At the first sign they can recognize themselves; at the other two, every discerning person can recognize them.

**D** First sign: While an enlightened person, thanks to a divine light, is simple and steady and free from self-observation <note>original: "ohne Beobachten"; likely referring to a state of being without self-conscious or analytical scrutiny of one's own progress</note>, these others are manifold and scattered, full of studying and pondering, and feel no inner unity nor image-free rest. By this they can recognize themselves.

### 46. How Christ was, is, and will always remain a lover of all

So that we may possess and desire this universal way <note>original: "allgemeine Weise"; referring to a state of being that is open and shared with all, rather than being focused on the self</note> above all the ways of which we have spoken (for it is the highest way), we wish to take Christ as our model. He was, is, and remains throughout eternity thoroughly universal <term>gemeinschaftlich</term> <note>original: "gemeinschaftlich"; a central concept in this spiritual tradition describing a person who is "common" or "universal," living for the benefit of the whole community rather than just themselves</note>, for he was sent to earth for the benefit of all who wish to turn toward him. It is true that he himself said he was sent only to the lost sheep of the house of Israel. However, this does not mean the Jews alone, but rather all those who shall behold God in eternity; these, and only these, belong to the house of Israel. For the Jews despised the Gospel, but the Gentiles <note>original: "Heiden"; literally "heathens," but referring here to the non-Jewish nations who accepted Christianity</note> entered in and accepted it. Through this, all Israel was preserved—that is to say, all the eternally chosen ones. 

**D** Now consider how Christ gave himself to all in true faithfulness. His inward, sublime prayer flowed out to his Father for everyone collectively who wishes to be saved. Christ was universal in his love, in his teaching, in his admonition, in gentle words of comfort, in merciful gifts, and in compassionate forgiveness. His soul and his body, his life and his death, as well as his service, were and are universal. Likewise his sacraments and his gifts. Christ never took food nor drink nor anything else for the body’s...

### 47. A rebuke for those who live in a disordered way from spiritual goods

Now, Christ has left his treasure and his income behind on earth. These are the seven sacraments and the outward goods of the Holy Church, which he earned through his death, and which should belong to all in common. And his servants who live from these goods should also be communal <term>gemeinschaftlich</term> <note>meaning to live for the collective good rather than personal gain</note>. All those who live on alms and belong to the spiritual estate should be communal—at least in prayer—and likewise all clergy and all who live in monasteries or hermitages <note>original: "Klausen"; the small, private dwellings of recluses or anchorites</note>. In the early days of the Holy Church and our faith, the popes, bishops, and priests were communal, for they converted the people, founded the Holy Church and our faith, and sealed this work with their blood and their death. ## 48. How Christ left himself for all in the Sacrament of the Altar.

Since I wish to especially praise and honor this way of life that embraces all in love, I find yet another jewel that Christ left in the Holy Church for all good people: in the Last Supper <note>original: "Abendmahl"</note> at the high Easter festival <note>Though "Osterfest" means Easter, the author refers to the events of the Last Supper and the beginning of the Passion</note>, when Christ wished to pass out of this misery to his Father after he had eaten the Paschal lamb with his disciples and the Old Covenant had come to an end. At the conclusion of the meal and the festival, he wished to give a special dish <note>original: "Zuspeise"; literally a side-dish or dessert, used here metaphorically to describe the Eucharist as a final, supreme gift</note>, something for which he had long yearned. With this, he intended to close the Old Covenant and begin the New Covenant. 

So he took bread into his holy, venerable hands and consecrated <note>The act of turning the bread and wine into the Body and Blood of Christ</note> his holy body, and thereafter his holy blood, and gave it to all his disciples; he left it for all good people without exception for their eternal salvation <note>original: "Seligkeit"; bliss or blessedness</note>. This gift and this "special dish" gladdens and adorns all high festivals and all banquets in heaven and on earth. 

In this gift, Christ gives himself to us in a threefold way: 
1. He gives us his flesh and blood and his transfigured bodily life, full of joy and sweetness. 
2. And he gives us his Spirit with its highest powers, full of glory and gifts, truth and justice. 
3. And he gives us his Personality <note>His divine personhood</note> with the divine clarity that carries away his spirit and all enlightened spirits into the high, blissful unity.

For in this way Christ received himself. <note>This likely refers to the theological idea that Christ, at the Last Supper, was the first to participate in the sacrament he instituted, acting as both the giver and the recipient.</note> And this desire cannot be great enough, for our nature receives his own nature—namely, the transfigured humanity of Christ, full of bliss and dignity. Therefore, I wish that a person, during this reception, would melt and dissolve away out of longing, joy, and bliss; for he receives and unites himself with the one who is the fairest, the most lovely, and the most <term>worthy of love</term> <note>original: "Minnelichste"; derived from the medieval word "Minne," which refers to a deep, often mystical or courtly love</note> among all the children of men.

In this glowing affection of love and in this desire, great salvation has often come to man, and many a secretly hidden wonder of God’s rich goodness has been uncovered and revealed. When a person, during this reception, is mindful of the <term>tortures</term> <note>original: "Martern"; refers to the physical torments of the Passion</note> and sufferings of the precious Body of Christ which he receives, he is sometimes overcome by such a fiery longing to surrender himself and such an intense <term>compassion</term> <note>original: "Mitleid"; literally "suffering-with," a central concept in Rhenish-German mysticism where the believer attempts to feel Christ's pain</note> that he desires to be nailed to the cross with Christ and wishes to shed his heart’s blood in honor of Christ. And he presses himself into the wounds and into the opened heart of Christ his Savior.

Through this practice, much has often been revealed to the person and much good has come to him. This sensible love, which arises from compassion, and the <term>imagination</term> <note>original: "Vorstellungskraft"; in this context, it refers to the spiritual faculty of visualizing Christ's life and suffering to provoke an emotional response</note> strongly stirred by inward contemplation of the wounds of Christ, can influence a person so much that it seems to him as if he feels the wounds and injuries of Christ in his heart and in all his limbs. And should indeed

<summary>This page describes the ecstatic and compassionate response of a believer receiving the Eucharist, emphasizing a desire to physically and spiritually share in Christ’s suffering and wounds. It highlights how intense meditation on the Passion can lead to a state where the believer feels Christ's injuries within their own body.</summary>

...if anyone were to receive the <term>stigmata</term> <note>original: "Wundmale"; the physical wounds of Christ's crucifixion appearing on a person's body</note> of the Lord in any way, it would certainly be such a person. And in this way, we satisfy Christ’s needs regarding the lower part of his humanity.

𝕯 We should also dwell within the unity of our spirit and flow outward with expansive love into heaven and earth with clear insight. In this way, we are similar to Christ according to the spirit and we satisfy him.

𝕯 Finally, through the power of Christ’s Personhood, by means of a simple intention and <term>fruitive love</term> <note>original: "genießender Liebe"; a technical mystical term for a love that finds rest and enjoyment in God himself rather than in His gifts</note>, we should go beyond ourselves and everything created within Christ and rest in our inheritance—that is, in the divine essence, for eternity. Christ wishes to grant us all of this spiritually every time we practice in this way and prepare ourselves for him within us. And he desires that we receive him both in the <term>Sacrament</term> <note>The Eucharist or Holy Communion</note> and spiritually, whenever it is fitting, appropriate, and reasonable. Even if a person does not always have such intense feelings and such longing—as long as they intend God’s praise and glory, their own progress, and their <term>blessedness</term> <note>original: "Seligkeit"; a state of supreme spiritual happiness or salvation</note>, they may go to the Table of our Lord without hindrance, provided their conscience is clear of <term>mortal sins</term> <note>original: "Todsünden"; in Catholic theology, grave sins that separate the soul from God's grace</note>.

## 49. Of the Unity of the Divine Nature and the Trinity of the Persons.

The high, <term>super-essential unity</term> <note>original: "überwesentliche Einheit"; refers to a divine reality that exists beyond the category of "being" or "substance" as understood by humans</note> of the divine nature, in which the Father and the Son have their <term>ground of nature</term> <note>original: "Naturgrund"; the foundational source from which their divine nature arises</note> in the unity of the Holy Spirit, lies beyond the grasp and understanding of all our powers within the bare essence of our spirit.

In this high stillness, God is set apart from all creatures within created light. This high unity of the divine nature is living and fruitful, because out of this same unity the eternal Word is born without ceasing from the Father. And through this birth, the Father knows the Son and all things in the Son. And the Son knows the Father and all things in the Father, for their nature is simple <note>original: "einfach"; in mystical theology, this refers to being "one-fold" or indivisible, meaning God has no separate parts</note>. From this mutual knowing of the Father and the Son in an eternal clarity flows an eternal pleasure and a groundless love <note>original: "grundlose Liebe"; a love that is its own foundation and has no external cause</note>, and that is the Holy Spirit. And through the Holy Spirit and the eternal wisdom, God inclines toward every single creature and bestows gifts upon and ignites in love each one according to their nobility and the state in which they were placed and called, thanks to their virtue and the eternal providence of God. And through this, all good spirits in heaven and on earth receive the impulse for the exercise of virtues and justice.

## 50. How God possesses and moves the soul naturally and supernaturally.

Now pay attention. I want to explain this to you through a parable. God has created the <term>highest heaven</term> <note>original: "obersten Himmel"; often called the Empyrean in medieval cosmology, it was believed to be a place of pure light and the dwelling of God</note>, a pure, simple clarity <note>original: "Klarheit"; a term used by mystics to describe the radiant, transparent brilliance of the divine presence</note> that surrounds and encloses all heavens and everything physical and material that God has ever created; for it is an outer dwelling and a kingdom of God and His saints, filled with glory and eternal joy. Since this heaven is an unmixed clarity...

<meta>This page continues the parable of the "highest heaven" (the Empyrean) as a metaphor for the soul. The previous page ended by describing the highest heaven as a place of pure, simple clarity. This page explains how God moves the soul through various spheres of influence, drawing a parallel between medieval cosmology—the movement of planets—and the internal movement of the Holy Spirit through the "seven gifts."</meta>

[clarity/simplicity] is, there is neither time, nor space, nor movement, and never any change, for this heaven is established and immutable above all things. The sphere that lies closest to the fire-blazing heaven is called the <term>First Mover</term> <note>original: "erste Bewegen"; in medieval astronomy, the *Primum Mobile* was the outermost sphere that moved all other celestial bodies</note>. Here, through the power of God, all movement originates from the highest heaven. By the force of this impulse, the firmament and all the planets receive their orbits, and all creatures, each in its own kind, receive life and growth. Now understand!

In a similar way, the essence of the soul is a spiritual kingdom of God, filled with divine clarity that transcends all our powers, except in the "Simple Way" <note>Ruysbroeck uses this term to describe a state of mystical union where the soul's active efforts cease and it simply exists in God</note>, about which I will remain silent here. Look, beneath this essence of the soul where God reigns, the unity of our spirit behaves exactly like the First Mover; for in this unity, the spirit is moved from above by the power of God, both naturally and supernaturally. For we have nothing of ourselves, neither in the natural nor in the supernatural order. 

And this movement of God, when it is supernatural, is the first and primary cause of all virtues. To certain enlightened people, through this impulse of God, the <term>seven gifts of the Holy Spirit</term> <note>Wisdom, Understanding, Counsel, Fortitude, Knowledge, Piety, and Fear of the Lord</note> are granted, which—just like seven planets—illuminate the whole life of the person and make it fruitful. This is the way in which God possesses the essential unity of our spirit as His kingdom, and how He works and flows out with gifts into the potential unity and into all our powers.

## 51. How Man Must Be Adorned to Receive the Most Inward Exercise.

Now consider how we can follow and possess the most inward exercise of our spirit in the created light. The person who is well-adorned with moral virtues in the "Active Life" <note>Ruysbroeck categorizes the spiritual journey into three stages: the Active Life (outward virtue), the Inward/Yearning Life (interior devotion), and the God-contemplative Life (union)</note> and has risen in nobility through "Inward Exercise," possesses the unity of their spirit in divine peace. Enlightened with supernatural wisdom, they flow out in generous love in heaven and on earth. They offer themselves and flow back with reverence and adoration into the same ground <note>original: "Grund"; a key term in Rhineland mysticism referring to the "soul-center" or the deepest point where the soul meets God</note> and into the high unity of God, from which all flowing originates. For every creature, the greater the gifts it has received from God, the more it has a constantly growing devotion of love and a more inward affection for its origin; for God and all his gifts summon us to union with him, and through love, virtue, and likeness, we seek to unite ourselves with him. 

Through the loving inclination of God and his inward working in the unity of our spirit, through our <term>ember-glow of love</term> <note>original: "Minneglut"; "Minne" is a medieval Germanic term for the reciprocal, often ecstatic love between the soul and God</note> and the complete immersion of all our powers into that same unity where God dwells, the "third coming" in the "Inward Exercise" arises. This is an internal touching or contact of Christ, in his divine clarity, in the innermost part of our spirit. The second coming, of which we have already spoken, we compared to a spring that flows out into three streams. But this third coming we wish

...we compare with the vein of the spring. For no stream is without a spring, and no spring is without a living vein. In the same way, the grace of God flows into the highest powers <note>Ruysbroeck refers to the "higher faculties" of the soul: memory, intellect, and will</note> like streams, and it drives and ignites a person toward all virtues. It acts like a spring within the unity of our spirit and falls into that same unity from which it originates, just like a living, bubbling vein from the living ground of divine richness, where there is never a lack of faithfulness and grace. 

This is the **touch** <note>original: "Berührung"; a technical term in mysticism for the direct, non-conceptual contact between God and the soul</note> that I mean. And the creature endures and suffers this touch, for here a union of the highest powers takes place within the unity of the spirit, above all the diversity of virtues. And here, out of free goodness, God alone works, who is the cause of all our virtues and our entire blessedness <note>original: "Seligkeit"; refers to the state of eternal joy and salvation</note>. 

In the unity of the spirit, where this vein bubbles up, one is raised above [human] action and reason, though not without reason. For enlightened reason and especially the **"Loving Power"** <term>Minnende Kraft: the soul's capacity for ecstatic, supernatural love for God</term> sense this touch. However, reason can neither grasp nor comprehend the manner of this touch, for it is a divine work, the source and beginning of all gifts and graces, and the final medium between God and the creature. And above this touch, in the silent essence of the spirit, hovers an incomprehensible clarity. This is the high Trinity, from which this touch proceeds. There God lives and reigns in the spirit, and the spirit in God.

## 52. Of the Third Coming of Christ and the Innermost Departure of Our Spirit through a Divine Touch.

Now Christ speaks inwardly within the spirit by means of this touching: "Go forth with exercises according to the manner of this touch." For this deep touch draws and compels our spirit toward the most intimate exercise that a creature can accomplish in a creaturely way within the **created light** <note>In mystical theology, "created light" refers to natural human reason and intellect, as opposed to the "uncreated light" of God's own essence</note>. Here the spirit rises, by means of the **"Loving Power"** <term>Minnende Kraft: the soul's innate capacity for supernatural love, which acts beyond human emotion or logic</term>, above all activity into that unity where this living vein of the touch wells up. And this touching calls upon the understanding to recognize God in His **clarity** <note>original: "Klarheit"; refers to the divine radiance or transfigured state of truth</note>, and it draws the **"Loving Power"** and invites it to enjoy God directly—which is exactly what the loving spirit desires above all else, both in the state of nature and of the **supernatural**.

By means of enlightened reason, the spirit rises in deep contemplation and gazes and searches into its deepest ground, where this touch resides. But here, reason and all created light are incapable of going further. For the clarity hovering above, which the touch brings about, blinds all created sight upon meeting it, because it is **bottomless** <note>original: "grundlos"; a technical term for the infinite "Abyss" of the Divine which has no shore or bottom for the human mind to grasp</note>. And every understanding, with its created light, behaves here like the **eyes of a bat** in the clarity of the sun. Nevertheless, the spirit is spurred on and called upon again and again, both by God and by itself, to fathom this touch and to grasp what God is and what this touch might be. And

<summary>This page describes the "Third Coming" of Christ as an internal "touch" that calls the soul to move beyond its own natural abilities and reason to encounter the infinite, blinding clarity of God.</summary>
<keywords>Third Coming of Christ, divine touch, Loving Power, mystical contemplation, abyss of God, created light, spiritual exercises</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Divine Touch" and the limits of human reason. It contrasts the inability of the intellect to grasp God’s essence with the soul's "Loving Power," which presses forward into the divine mystery even when understanding is left behind.</meta>

Enlightened **reason** <note>original: "Vernunft"</note> asks itself time and again where this might come from, and repeatedly conducts investigations to trace the **vein of honey** <note>original: "Honigader"; a metaphor for the sweet, nourishing flow of divine grace</note> back to its source. However, in this matter, reason is as wise on the first day as it will ever be. Therefore, reason and all deliberation say: “I do not know what it is.” For the **divine clarity** <note>original: "göttliche Klarheit"; the transfigured radiance of God's presence</note> hovering above repels every understanding and blinds it upon meeting. Thus God, in His clarity, remains exalted above all spirits, both in heaven and on earth. 

But those who, by means of virtues and inward practice, have dug ever deeper until they reached the origin of their **ground** <note>original: "Grund"; the deepest, most essential part of the soul where the human meets the Divine</note>—that is, the door of eternal life—they can feel the touch. There, the clarity of God shines so strongly that reason and every understanding cannot go further; they must pass by God’s incomprehensible clarity and yield before it. 

Even if the spirit feels this in its ground, and though reason and understanding must halt before the divine clarity and remain outside the gate, the **"Loving Power"** <term>Minnende Kraft: the soul's innate capacity for supernatural love, which acts beyond human emotion or logic</term> still wishes to go further. For it is called upon and invited just as the understanding is, yet it is blind and desires to **enjoy** <note>original: "genießen"; in mystical theology, this refers to the "fruition" or direct, experiential union with God</note>. For enjoyment consists more in tasting and feeling than in understanding. Therefore, love wishes to proceed even where the understanding must stay outside.

Now Christ speaks inwardly in the spirit by means of this touch:

And every understanding with its created light behaves here like the eyes of a bat in the clarity of the sun. <meta>Continuing from page 132-133, this page completes the thought that while reason stops at the gate of divine mystery, the "Loving Power" is never satisfied. It explores the central paradox of the mystical life: the more the soul experiences God's sweetness, the hungrier it becomes, because the finite soul cannot fully contain the infinite God.</meta>

...is never satisfied; namely, an inward desire and striving of the <term>Loving Power <note>original: "Minnende Kraft"; the faculty of the soul that moves toward God through love rather than intellect</note></term> and the created spirit for an uncreated Good. Since the spirit wishes to enjoy [God] and is summoned and invited by God to do so, it absolutely intends to reach this goal.

Behold, here begins an eternal desire and a striving forward in eternal deprivation. These are the poorest people that exist, for they are hungry and greedy and suffer from a <term>voracious hunger <note>original: "Gähnhunger"; literally a "yawning" or "gaping hunger," describing a spiritual void that feels like an open mouth that cannot be filled by anything created</note></term>. No matter how they eat and drink, they never become full at this stage, for this hunger is eternal. For a created vessel cannot contain an uncreated Good within itself.

Therefore, there is an eternally hungry wrestling here, but God towers above all this in His <term>withholding <note>original: "Nicht-Gewähren"; God's refusal to be fully "possessed" or "contained" by the finite human spirit, which keeps the spiritual desire active and alive</note></term>. Here there are many dishes of food and drink, of which no one knows except the one who enjoys them; but full satisfaction in that enjoyment is the very dish that is not granted, which is why the hunger always renews itself.

To be sure, at the touch [of God], <term>streams of honey <note>original: "Honigbäche"; a continuation of the "honey-vein" imagery from the previous page, representing the sweetness of divine grace and spiritual delight</note></term> full of every delight flow forth, for the spirit tastes this delight in every conceivable way it can devise; yet it all remains of a created nature and remains beneath God. Hence the eternal hunger and the restlessness. If God were to bestow upon such a person all the gifts of all the saints, and in addition everything He could possibly offer, except for Himself—the gaping desire of the spirit would remain hungry and unquenched. God’s inward touching and moving makes us hungry and awakens our desire; for the Spirit of God hunts our spirit: the [more]...

# 54. Concerning a loving strife between God’s Spirit and our spirit.

In this storm of love, two spirits contend: the Spirit of God and our spirit. God inclines toward us through the Holy Spirit, and through this, we are touched in love. And through the activity of God and through the <term>Loving Power <note>original: "Minnende Kraft"; the faculty of the soul that reaches for God when the intellect reaches its limit</note></term>, our spirit presses and inclines itself into God, and through this, God is touched. From these two arises the <term>strife of love <note>original: "Liebesstreit"; a common mystical paradox where the soul and God are seen as competing in their generosity and desire for one another</note></term> in that deepest encounter, and in this most intimate and fiery meeting, each spirit is most deeply wounded by love. These two spirits—that is, our spirit and God’s Spirit—shine and radiate, one into the other, and each shows the other its face. This causes the spirits to constantly strive toward one another in love. Each demands from the other what he is, and each gives and offers to the other what he is. This causes the lovers to flow into one another. God’s touching and His giving, our striving in love...

<summary>This page describes the "Life of Love" as a state beyond reason where the soul and God engage in a "loving strife," a mutual and deepening encounter of desire and self-giving. It emphasizes that while this union feels inseparable, the human experience of it continues to grow and evolve throughout life.</summary>

and our returning of that love; this is what keeps love awake. This flowing and counter-flowing causes the <term>fountain of love <note>original: "Minnequelle"; "Minne" is the Middle High German term for the reciprocal, mystical love between the soul and God</note></term> to overflow; and thus God’s touching and our striving for love become a single, simple love. Here the human being becomes possessed by love, so that he must forget himself and God and knows only how to love. In this way, the spirit is consumed by heat in the fire of love and enters so deeply into God’s touch that every striving within him is overcome. He is brought to nothing in all his doing, exhausts himself, and—beyond all mere affection—becomes love itself. Thus, above all virtues, he possesses the innermost essence of his <term>created nature <note>original: "Geschaffenheit"; the core of the soul where it was first shaped by the Creator</note></term>, in which all creaturely activity begins and ends. This is love in itself, the foundation and the ground of all virtues.

## 61. The Encounter Between God and Our Spirit in Unity and Likeness.

Now God gazes upon the dwelling and the rest which He has created with us and within us—that is, the <term>unity <note>original: "Einheit"; the deep, unchanging center of the soul where it is one with God's own being</note></term> and the <term>likeness <note>original: "Ähnlichkeit"; the active part of the soul that mirrors God through holy living and character</note></term>. And He desires to visit this unity without ceasing, by means of the new arrival of His <term>noble birth <note>original: "hohen Geburt"; a key mystical concept referring to the eternal "birth" of the Son of God (the Word) within the human soul</note></term> and by means of the rich outflowing of His <term>fathomless love <note>original: "grundlosen Liebe"; literally "groundless," meaning a love so deep it has no bottom, or a love that exists for no other reason than itself</note></term>; for He desires to dwell in delight within the loving spirits. 

And He desires to visit the likeness of our spirit with rich gifts, so that we may become more like Him and more enlightened in the virtues. Now, Christ desires that we dwell and remain in the <insert>essential</insert> unity of our spirit, rich in Him, standing above all created activity and above all virtues; yet He also desires that we remain active within that same unity, rich and filled with virtues and heavenly gifts. And He wants us to seek out this unity and likeness unceasingly with every work that we do; for in every new moment, God is born within us, and from this noble birth, the Holy Spirit flows forth with all His gifts. Now we must meet the gifts of God by means of our likeness, but we must meet that noble birth within our [essential] unity.

<summary>Ruysbroeck explains that salvation requires "mediation" through grace and virtue. He describes a mystical paradox where the soul must both rest in God's essential unity and remain actively engaged in a virtuous life (likeness).</summary>

### 62. How we should encounter God in all our actions.

Consider now how we should encounter God in every work, increase in greater <term>likeness <note>original: "Verähnlichung"; the process of the soul becoming more like God through virtue and grace.</note></term>, and possess the <term>enjoying unity <note>original: "genießende Einheit"; often translated as "fruitive unity," referring to the state where the soul does not just work for God but rests in the blissful enjoyment of Him.</note></term> in an even nobler way. Every good work, however insignificant it may be, that we direct toward God with love and with a pure, <term>simple intention <note>original: "einfältiger Meinung"; in this context, "simple" means "single-minded" or "undivided," referring to a focus solely on God without selfish motives.</note></term>, attains for us greater likeness and eternal life in God. The simple intention draws the scattered powers together into the unity of the spirit and connects the spirit with God. 

The simple intention is the end, the beginning, and the adornment of all virtues. It offers up praise, honor, and all virtues to God, and it passes beyond itself, beyond all heavens and all things, and finds God in the <term>simple ground <note>original: "einfachen Grund"; the "ground" of the soul is the deepest, most essential part of a human being where the Spark of the Divine dwells.</note></term> of itself. That intention is simple which, in all things, intends nothing but God, directed toward God. 

The simple intention drives out hypocrisy and falsehood. And man should cultivate and practice it in all his doing, more than anything else, for it keeps man in God's presence, with a clear mind, zealously concerned for virtue, and free from <term>strange fear <note>original: "fremder Furcht"; likely referring to worldly anxieties or the "slavish fear" of punishment, as opposed to the "holy fear" born of love.</note></term>, both here below and on the Day of Judgment. 

The simple intention is the "single eye" of which Christ speaks, which keeps the whole body—that is, man's entire conduct and his whole life—radiant and pure from sins. The simple intention is the spirit's inward, enlightened, <term>loving <note>original: "minnigliche"; related to the Middle High German "Minne," a deep, spiritual, and often ecstatic love.</note></term> inclination; it is the foundation of all spirituality. In it is faith, hop—

And God is sufficient to each according to their particular nature, and everyone seeks God in the ground of their spirit, according to the measure of their love, here below and in eternity.

...of all virtues and all holiness, namely how we must approach God in <term>likeness <note>original: "Ähnlichkeit"; the mystical concept that through virtue, the human soul becomes a mirror reflecting the Divine character.</note></term>, so that we may then rest with Him in <term>unity <note>original: "Einheit"; the ultimate state of mystical union where the soul is one with God's essence.</note></term>. ¶ When a person lives in the fear of God, in moral virtues and outward spiritual exercises, obedient and subject to the Holy Church and the commandments of God; when he is willing and ready for all good things with a <term>simple intention <note>original: "einfältiger Meinung"; a single-minded focus on God, free from selfish motives.</note></term>, then he is like God through faithfulness and the conformity of his will—both in action and in restraint—according to the will of God. And he rests in God beyond likeness; for through faithfulness and simple intention, he fulfills the will of God to a greater or lesser degree, according to the measure of his likeness, and through love, he rests in his Beloved beyond that likeness. ¶ And if he practices well in what he has received from God, then God gives him the Spirit of Goodness and <term>Mercy <note>original: "Mildtätigkeit"; in Ruysbroeck's framework, this refers to a specific spiritual disposition of kindness.</note></term>.¹ Thus he becomes merciful, gentle of heart, and kind. By this, he is more alive and more [God-]like, and he feels that he rests deeper in God, and that the life of virtue has expanded and deepened within him. And this likeness and rest taste all the better to him the more like God he becomes. ¶ And if he practices well on this level with great zeal and with a simple intention, struggling against that which opposes the individual virtues, he receives the third gift, namely that of <term>Knowledge <note>original: "Wissenschaft"; here meaning spiritual insight or "scientia," one of the seven gifts of the Holy Spirit.</note></term> and <term>Discernment <note>original: "Unterscheidung"; the ability to distinguish between divine promptings and worldly distractions.</note></term>. By this, he becomes rational and recognizes what he must do...
