...and must let go, and where he should give or take. 
And through <term>simple intention <note>original: "einfältige Meinung"; a single-minded focus on God, free from selfish motives.</note></term> and divine love, such a person rests in God above himself in unity. 
And he possesses himself in <term>likeness <note>original: "Ähnlichkeit"; the mystical state where the human soul reflects the character of God.</note></term> and all his deeds with greater delight, for he is obedient and subject to the Father, reasonable and discerning toward the Son, and charitable and kind toward the Holy Spirit. 
And so he bears a likeness to the Holy Trinity and rests in God through his love and the simplicity of his intention. 
And in this consists the whole **"Active Life."** 

So a person must practice with great diligence and pursue his simple intention with insight. 
And he must guard against everything that contradicts virtue and always lay himself at the feet of Christ in humility. 
Then he increases hourly in virtue and likeness, and if he remains steadfast, he cannot go astray. 

Yet for all this, he does not go beyond the **"Active Life."** 
For if a person, in his practice, clings more to the changing stirrings of his heart and the variety of his works than to their ground and their goal; and if his practice rests more on the visible aspects of the holy <term>means of grace <note>original: "Gnadenmittel"; the sacraments and rituals through which divine favor is believed to be channeled.</note></term>, on signs and customs, than on their foundation and the truth they signify: then he remains always an outward person. 
He will, however, because of his good works practiced with a simple intention, be saved.

# 64. Of the Gift of Strength.

If, therefore, a person wishes to approach God and raise their practice and their life to a higher level, then they must penetrate from the works to the "why," and from the outward signs to the truth. Only then do they become master of their works and a knower of the truth, and thus attain the <term>Inward Life <note>original: "Inniges Leben"; a stage of mystical development where the focus shifts from external religious duties to an internal, heart-centered relationship with the Divine.</note></term>. 

And God gives them the fourth gift, namely the <term>Spirit of Strength <note>original: "Geist der Stärke"; traditionally called the gift of Fortitude, it provides the spiritual courage needed to remain constant in faith despite worldly changes.</note></term>; now they can overcome joy and sorrow, gain and loss, hopes and anxieties concerning earthly things, all kinds of obstacles, and the distractions of multiplicity. And so the person becomes free and liberated from all created things. 

When a person is <term>imageless <note>original: "bilderlos"; a state of mind where one lets go of sensory images and mental concepts to experience God more directly.</note></term>, they have mastery over themselves, and they are easily and effortlessly unified and made inward. They turn freely and without hindrance toward God with heartfelt devotion, with high desire, with thanks and praise, and with <term>simple intention <note>original: "einfältige Meinung"; a single-minded focus on God, stripped of selfish motives or secondary goals.</note></term>. 

Thus they find a relish in everything they do and in their whole life, both inwardly and outwardly; for they stand before the throne of the Holy Trinity, and God often grants them inner comfort and sweetness. For whoever serves at such a table with thanks, praise, and heartfelt reverence often drinks of the wine and eats of the crumbs and the pieces that fall from the Lord’s table. And they possess a lasting inner peace, thanks to the simplicity of their intention. 

As soon as they desire to persevere before God in thanks and praise and in pure intention, the gift of strength becomes twofold within them. For they do not lose themselves (as the...

### 65. Of the Gift of Counsel.

If now no created being can violate or hinder the person so that they remain in their <term>simple upward-striving intention <note>original: "einfältige aufwärtsstrebende Meinung"; a single-minded, pure desire to reach God without being distracted by secondary goals or personal gain.</note></term>—if they remain steadfast in the praise of God, and seek and intend God above all His gifts by virtue of that strength—then God bestows upon them the fifth gift, which is that of <term>Counsel <note>original: "Rat"; traditionally, the gift that helps the soul distinguish between its own will and the prompting of the Holy Spirit.</note></term>. In this gift, the Father draws the person from within and invites them to His right hand into His unity with the <term>elect <note>original: "Auserwählten"; those chosen or set apart for a closer relationship with the Divine.</note></term>. And the Son speaks spiritually to them: "Follow me to my Father; <term>only one thing is necessary <note>original: "eines nur ist notwendig"; a reference to Luke 10:42, where Jesus tells Martha that Mary has chosen the "better part" or the "one thing" needed: contemplative focus on God.</note></term>." And the Holy Spirit causes the heart to open and glow in burning love. And from this arises a passionate <insert>life of love</insert> and an inner restlessness; for whoever follows this counsel is seized by a <insert>storm of love</insert>, and only God can...

### 66. Of the first degree of the Gift of Understanding.

And for this reason, God gives him the sixth gift, namely the <term>Spirit of Understanding <note>The Gift of Understanding allows the human mind to penetrate the deeper meaning of divine truths and the mysteries of faith, going beyond simple intellectual knowledge.</note></term>. We previously compared this gift to a fountain with three streams, for it strengthens our spirit in unity, it reveals the truth, and it produces a wide, all-encompassing love. This gift may also be compared to sunshine, for the sun, through its light, fills the air with a simple brightness; it illuminates all forms and shows the distinction between all colors. In this way, it makes its own power known, while its warmth benefits the whole world, bringing utility and fertility. In a similar manner, the first radiance of this gift brings about simplicity in the spirit. And this simplicity is shone through with a special clarity, just like the air of the heavens is by the light of the sun; for the grace of God, which is the foundation of all other gifts, dwells by its essence as a simple light in our <term>possible intellect <note>original: "möglichen Verstande"; a term from medieval scholastic philosophy (intellectus possibilis) referring to the mind's capacity to receive and be shaped by knowledge or divine illumination.</note></term>, and through this simple light, our spirit is strengthened, simply enlightened, and filled with graces and divine gifts; and here it is similar to God.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the soul, through the first radiance of the Gift of Understanding, finds a balance between active love and contemplative rest. It concludes a thought from the previous page regarding how the soul possesses itself in love.</meta>

...more rest, and the more rest, the more inward the love. For the one lives in the other, and he who does not love does not rest; and he who does not rest does not love. Yet, it seems to this or that good person as if he does not love God, nor rest in Him. But this perception comes precisely from love. Because he desires to love more than he is able, it seems to him that he does too little. Yet in this, he tastes love and rest, for no one can understand how one loves through action and rests through enjoyment except the <term>yielded <note>original: *gelassene*. A central concept in German mysticism (*Gelassenheit*), meaning a state of surrender, detachment, and letting go of the self-will to allow God to act within.</note></term>, <term>unencumbered <note>original: *ledige*. Meaning free or empty of worldly attachments and self-centered distractions.</note></term>, and enlightened person. 

In the meantime, every lover is one with God in rest and similar to Him in the works of love; for God, in His high nature—whose image we bear—is, according to His <term>essential unity <note>original: *Wesenseinheit*. The oneness of the divine essence, beyond the distinction of the Persons.</note></term>, enjoying in eternal rest, and is active in eternal activity according to the <term>Trinity <note>original: *Dreiheit*.</note></term>. One is the perfection of the other, for resting lies in the unity and acting in the Trinity; and so both remain for eternity. Therefore, if man is to taste God, he must love, and if he wishes to love, he can taste [Him]. But if he allows himself to be satisfied with other things, he cannot taste what God is. And therefore we must possess ourselves, simply in virtues and likeness, and we must possess God above ourselves by means of love in rest and in union. And that is the first point, namely how a <term>common man <note>original: *gemeinschaftlicher Mensch*. In this mystical tradition, a "common" or "universal" person is one who has become so aligned with God that they are equally ready for both the "rest" of contemplation and the "activity" of serving others, just as God is both rest and action.</note></term> is established.

### 68. Of the third degree of the gift of understanding.

To the degree that the air is enlightened by the light of the sun, the heat also increases and brings forth general fruitfulness. As soon as our reason and our understanding are enlightened in such a way that they recognize divine truth with discernment, then the will—that is, the <term>Loving Power <note>original: *Minnende Kraft*. From the Middle High German 'minne', a specific term for the reciprocal, ecstatic love between the soul and God.</note></term>—is ignited into a rich outpouring in faithfulness and love.

over all people. For this gift establishes in us a wide, all-encompassing love, by virtue of the knowledge of the truth which is granted to us from the light of this gift. For the most <term>simple <note>original: *Einfältigsten*. In this mystical tradition, simplicity refers to a soul that is focused solely on God, free from the distractions of multiple desires.</note></term> are the most <term>serene <note>original: *Gelassensten*. This refers to *Gelassenheit*, a central concept in German mysticism meaning a state of detached, peaceful surrender to God's will.</note></term>, those most satisfied within themselves, and they are most deeply sunken into God; they are the most enlightened in understanding and the most manifold in good works and the most <term>universal <note>original: *Gemeinschaftlichsten*. Refers to the "common man" (as described on the previous page) who, like God, is available to everyone and serves the common good without self-interest.</note></term> in outflowing love. And they are the least hindered, for they are most like God. For He is simplicity according to His essence and clarity according to His understanding, and He is the outflowing, all-encompassing love in His works. And the more we are like God in these three, the more intimately we are united with Him. And therefore we must remain simple in our <term>ground <note>original: *Grund*. The "ground of the soul" is the deepest part of the human spirit where the mystic believes God dwells directly.</note></term>, contemplate all things with enlightened reason, and flood everything with all-encompassing love, just as the sun of heaven remains within itself—that is, simple and immutable—even though its light and its warmth benefit the entire world. 

¶ Now hear how we must live with enlightened understanding in all-encompassing love. The Father is, according to essence and person, the beginning of all Divinity. Therefore we should bow ourselves in spirit before the majesty of the Father in humble reverence: through this we possess <term>humility</term>, which is the foundation of all virtues. We should worship inwardly, that is, offer honor and glorification to the omnipotence of the Father: through this we are spiritually elevated, for in His power He created all things from nothing and sustains them. We should [offer] praise and

<meta>This page continues the practical application of the Gift of Understanding. We should dedicate thanks and eternal service to the faithfulness and the love of God, who has freed us from the bonds of the enemy <note>The "enemy" refers to the devil or the personification of evil and temptation.</note> and from eternal death: through this, we become free. We should present before the Wisdom of God and lament the blindness and ignorance of human nature, and we should desire that all people be enlightened and come to know the truth: through this, God is recognized and honored by them. We should call upon the mercy of God for sinners, so that they may be converted and advance in the <term>virtues <note>In this context, virtues are not just good habits but spiritual strengths that align the soul with God's will.</note></term>: through this, God is longingly loved by them.

We should generously give from God's rich goodness to all those who are in need, so that they all may be filled <insert>with gifts</insert> and <insert>so enriched</insert> flow back into God: through this, they all become partakers of God. We should also offer to the Father in Christ Jesus all the heartfelt devotion of the angels, the saints, and all good people: through this, we are united with them all in the worship of God. We should also sacrifice to the Father all the service of the Holy Church and the exalted <term>Sacrifice of the Mass <note>The Eucharist, viewed here as a universal offering that the mystic participates in spiritually.</note></term> of all priests, and everything we can accomplish and recognize, in the name of Christ, so that we may encounter God through Christ and become like Him in all-encompassing love, and so that we may <term>simply <note>original: *einfältiglich*. This relates to the concept of "simplicity" from the previous page, meaning a state of being focused purely on the Divine essence.</note></term> grow beyond all likeness and unite with Him in the <term>essential unity <note>original: *wesenhaften Einheit*. This is the highest state of mystical union where the soul, while remaining itself, experiences a oneness with God's own being.</note></term>.

### 69. Concerning the Gift of Wisdom. Now attend!

Upon returning into ourselves, the enjoying unity of God behaves exactly like a darkness, a <term>wayless state <note>original: *Unweise*. A technical term in Ruysbroeck’s mysticism referring to a state beyond all human modes, forms, or methods—where God is experienced in His simple, incomprehensible nature.</note></term>, and an incomprehensibility. In this loving return, the seventh gift arises: the spirit of <term>savoring wisdom <note>original: *auskostenden Weisheit*. It permeates the simplicity of our spirit, soul, and body with wisdom and spiritual taste. This is a spiritual touching or stirring in the unity of our spirit; it is the outpouring and the ground of all graces, all gifts, and all virtues. And in this touch of God, each person tastes their striving and their life, according to the strength of this touch and the measure of their love. And this divine touching is the most intimate mediator between God and us, between resting and working, between the <term>mode <note>original: *Weise*. The specific, structured ways of performing virtues or religious duties.</note></term> and the <term>wayless</term>, between time and eternity. it is recognized and felt by us last of all. For when we have sought God lovingly in all spiritual exercises into the innermost part of our <term>essential ground <note>original: *Wesensgrund*. For even if the air is illuminated by sunlight, and even if our eyes are sharp and healthy: as soon as we wish to follow the rays that bring the light and observe the sun's disk, the eyes must give up their activity and can only <term>passively <note>original: *leidend*. For if we could receive God into our capacity for comprehension, He would give Himself to us directly. But that is impossible for us, for we are too narrow and too small to grasp Him. And therefore He pours His gifts into us according to the measure of our capacity and according to the nobility of our spiritual exercises. For the fruitful unity of God rests always above the unity of our powers and demands of us at all times a likeness in love and virtues. There—

<meta>This page continues the description of the mystical union with God, transitioning from the "suffering" or passivity of the soul to the resulting state of perpetual hunger and spiritual renewal. Here we are touched anew every hour, so that we may renew ourselves every hour and become more alike [to God] in virtues. And through this renewed touching, the spirit falls into hunger and thirst; it desires to taste the full <term>abyss <note>original: *Abgründlichkeit*. Refers to the "bottomless" or unfathomable depth of the divine nature.</note></term> [of God] and to race through it in a <term>storm of love <note>original: *Minnesturm*. *Minne* is the Middle High German and Middle Dutch term for mystical, courtly, or divine love. A "storm of love" denotes an intense, overwhelming spiritual desire that drives the soul toward God.</note></term>, so that it may be satisfied. 

And from this arises an eternal, hungering striving and a state of remaining unfulfilled; for all loving spirits desire and strive after God, each according to the rank of his nobility and according to how he is touched by God. Nevertheless, God remains eternally unreachable to the mode of our active desiring, and therefore there remains in us an eternal hunger and an eternally desiring <term>inward turning <note>original: *Einkehren*. A mystical term for retreating from the external world into the interior of the soul to find God.</note></term> [into God] along with all the saints. 

And in the encounter with God, the clarity and the heat are so great and so immeasurable that all spirits must give up their activity and, in felt love, dissolve and vanish into the unity of their spirit. And there, as mere creatures, they must <term>suffer <note>original: *erleiden*. As noted on the previous page, this refers to being passive or receptive to God's action rather than performing one's own efforts.</note></term> the <term>in-working <note>original: *Einwirken*. The direct operation or influence of God within the soul.</note></term> of God. 

And here, our spirit, the grace of God, and all our virtues are one felt love without activity; for our spirit has exhausted its own working and has itself [become] love. And there the spirit is <term>simple <note>original: *einfältig*. Not meaning "unintelligent," but "single-folded" or unified, stripped of multiplicity and distractions.</note></term>, receptive to all gifts and fit for all virtues. And in this ground of felt love lives the <term>bubbling vein <note>original: *sprudelnde Ader*. A metaphor for a fountain or spring of divine life constantly flowing into the soul.</note></term>—that is, the <term>irradiation <note>original: *Einstrahlung*. The inflowing light or "streaming in" of God.</note></term> or the in-working of God—which moves us, stirs us, draws us in every hour, and lets us flow out again in new works of virtue. With this, I have shown you the ground and the manner of all virtues.

# 70. Concerning the Highest Degree of the Most Inward Life.

Now understand! The immeasurable illumination of God, joined with incomprehensible light, which is the cause of all gifts and all virtues—this same incomprehensible light transforms and permeates the <term>enjoying inclination <note>original: *genießende Neigung*. In mystical theology, this refers to the soul's natural, affective pull toward finding its rest and delight in the Divine.</note></term> of our spirit with <term>un-way <note>original: *Unweise*. A technical term in Rhineland and Flemish mysticism (related to the Dutch *onwise*) referring to a state that transcends all human methods, forms, or intellectual categories—a "wayless" way.</note></term>, which is to say, with incomprehensible light. And in this light, the spirit sinks away from itself into enjoying rest; for this rest is <term>without way <note>original: *weiselos*. Lacking a defined manner, limit, or human form.</note></term> and bottomless, and it cannot be known except through itself—namely, through resting. For if we could know and understand it, it would then take on a manner and a measure. But then it could not satisfy us, and the rest would become an eternal unrest.

And therefore, the simple, sunken, <term>loving <note>original: *minnigliche*. From the word *Minne*, which denotes a deep, often ecstatic, sacred love.</note></term> inclination of our spirit produces within us an enjoying love; and enjoying love is bottomless. And the <term>abyss <note>original: *Abgrund*. A central concept in this tradition, representing the unfathomable depth of both the soul and God, where the two meet in union.</note></term> calls out to the abyss—that is, into all those who are united with the Spirit of God in enjoying love. This calling-into is an overflowing of the <term>essential light <note>original: *Wesenslicht*. The light emanating from God’s very essence or being, rather than a created light like the sun or human reason.</note></term>, and this essential light causes us—within the embrace of a bottomless love—to lose ourselves and flow out into the <term>wild darkness <note>original: *wilde Finsternis*. A common mystical paradox: God is so "bright" that the human intellect perceives Him as darkness; "wild" here suggests a state that is untamed and beyond human order.</note></term> of the Godhead. And thus, in immediate union, one with the Spirit of God, we can encounter God through God and, with Him and in Him, possess our eternal <term>blessedness <note>original: *Seligkeit*. The state of supreme spiritual happiness and union with the Divine.</note></term> perpetually.

### 72. Concerning the second way of the most inward exercise.

Sometimes such an inward person turns toward God longingly and actively, in order to bring God honor and **glorification** <note>original: *Verherrlichung*.</note> and to sacrifice and consume themselves and everything they can accomplish in the love of God. A "holy unrest" or spiritual hunger that cannot be stilled by anything created.</note>. ## 73. On the Third Way of the Most Inward Exercise.

From these two ways flows the third way, namely an inward life according to **justice** <note>original: *Gerechtigkeit*. In this mystical context, justice refers to a state of spiritual "rightness" or balance, where the soul renders to God what is due through both action and stillness.</note>. Now understand: God comes into us without ceasing, both mediately and immediately, and demands from us both **savoring** <note>original: *Genießen*. Often translated as "fruition," this refers to the soul’s joyful experience of God's presence.</note> and **working** <note>original: *Wirken*. The active practice of virtues and deeds of love.</note>, and requires that the one should not be hindered by the other, but rather always be strengthened. And therefore, the inward person possesses their life in these two ways, namely in resting and in working. 

And in each of these, the person is whole and undivided; for they are wholly in God while they rest in savoring, and they are wholly in themselves while they actively <term>love</term> <note>original: *minnt*. From the Middle High German *Minne*, a term for the intense, reciprocal, and mystical love between the soul and God.</note>; and at all times they are called upon and admonished by God to renew both—the resting and the working. And the justice of the spirit desires to pay hourly what God demands of it, and therefore, with every **irradiation** <note>original: *Einstrahlung*. A divine "beaming in" or illumination from God into the soul.</note> from God, the spirit turns inward, working and savoring, and thus it is renewed in all virtues and sunk deeper into savoring rest. 

For God gives Himself and His gifts in one single giving, and the spirit gives itself and all its works in every inward turning. For through the simple irradiation of God and through the savoring inclination and the **out-flowing** <note>original: *Entflossenheit*. The state of the soul being "poured out" in love toward the Divine.</note> of love, the spirit is united with God and is continually led over into rest. And through the gift of understanding and of savoring wisdom, the spirit is stirred to activity and enlightened hourly and set aflame in love. And everything that one can desire is shown and held before the person in the spirit. They are hungry and thirsty, for they behold the **food of angels** <note>original: *Engel Speise*. A traditional reference to the Eucharist or to God Himself as the ultimate nourishment of the soul.</note>...

<summary>This page describes the third stage of inward spiritual life, defined by "justice," which is the perfect balance between active love (working) and contemplative rest (savoring God).</summary>
<keywords>justice, savoring, working, resting, irradiation, out-flowing of love, food of angels, mystical union</keywords>

and heavenly drink. He exerts himself in the **zeal of love**, for he beholds his rest. He is a pilgrim and beholds his homeland. He fights in love for the victory, for he sees his crown. Consolation, peace, joy, beauty, wealth, and everything that can gladden is shown to the **enlightened reason** <note>original: *erleuchteten Vernunft*. In this mystical tradition, this refers to the human intellect when it is illuminated by divine grace to see spiritual truths.</note> in God, beyond measure in spiritual images. 

And by virtue of this showing and through **God's touch** <note>original: *Gottes Berührung*. A technical term for the direct, immediate influence of God upon the soul's essence.</note>, love remains in activity. For such a **righteous person** <note>original: *Gerechter*. This refers to a person living in a state of spiritual "rightness" or "justice" (Gerechtigkeit), which is the primary theme of this section.</note> has, through **resting and working**, established a true life in the spirit that will always endure. However, after this life, it will pass into a higher state. 

Thus, the person is righteous and is on the way to God with inward love in eternal working; and he enters into God by means of the savoring inclination, in eternal rest. And he remains in God and yet goes out to all creatures in all-encompassing love, in **virtues** and in **justice**. And that is the highest stage of the **inward life**. 

All those who do not possess **resting and working** in one practice have not attained this justice. That righteous person cannot be hindered in his turning inward, for he turns inward both savoring and acting. Rather, he resembles a **double mirror**, which receives images on both sides. For according to the highest part [of his spirit], the person receives God together with all His gifts, and according to the lowest part, he takes in bodily images through the senses. Now he can hold inwardness whenever he wishes and practice justice without hindrance; however, the person is changeable in this <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing to explain the human susceptibility to change or distraction despite this high spiritual state.</note>

<summary>This page defines the highest stage of the inward life as a state of "justice," where the soul balances eternal rest in God with active love toward all creatures. ## 74. How certain people live in contradiction to these three ways.

Now there are people who appear good, yet live contrary to these three ways and all virtues. Therefore, let everyone explore and examine themselves. Every person who is neither drawn by God nor enlightened is not touched by love; he knows neither **active love** <term>tätige Liebe: Love expressed through outward deeds and spiritual exercises.</term> [combined] with longing, nor the simple, loving inclination in **savoring rest** <note>original: *genießendem Ruhen*. A technical term in mysticism for the soul’s quiet enjoyment of God’s presence, often called "fruitive rest."</note>. And therefore he cannot unite himself with God, for all who live without **supernatural love** <note>original: *übernatürliche Liebe*. Love that is a gift from God (grace), rather than a natural human emotion.</note> incline toward themselves and seek rest in things foreign to God. For all creatures are by nature inclined toward rest; and for this reason, rest is sought by both the good and the evil in many different ways. 

Now take note: when a person is bare and **image-less** <note>original: *bildlos*. A state of meditation where the mind is emptied of sensory thoughts, memories, and mental pictures to create a void for God to fill.</note> according to the senses and idle, without activity according to the **highest powers** <note>original: *obersten Kräften*. The superior faculties of the soul: memory, intellect, and will.</note>, he attains rest in a purely natural way. And all creatures can find this rest and possess it within themselves in a purely natural state without the impulse of **God’s grace**, if only they can empty themselves of images and detach themselves from every activity. However, the loving person can in this—

### 75. Of those who live in opposition to the second way.

Now understand: If a person seeks to cultivate rest in **idleness** <term>Ledigkeit: A state of being empty or unoccupied; here, it refers to a dangerous spiritual vacuum where a person stops the active, loving pursuit of God in favor of a blank state of mind.</term> in any way, without a deep and longing **turning toward God** <note>original: *Hinkehr zu Gott*. A focused orientation of the soul's will and desire toward the Divine.</note>, he is exposed to every error. This is because he has turned away from God and, through **natural love** <note>original: *natürliche Liebe*. In this context, it refers to human affection and desire that is driven by one's own nature and ego, rather than being transformed by divine grace.</note>, has turned toward himself, seeking and desiring comfort, sweetness, and whatever he happens to crave. 

Such a person is like a **merchant**, for in all his works he is focused on himself; he seeks and intends his own rest and his own profit more than the glory of God. A person who lives this way, in purely natural love, always possesses only himself, without self-surrender, in **self-love** <note>original: *Eigenliebe*. A term for egoism or selfishness, specifically regarding one's spiritual status.</note>. 

And one person or another may lead a harsh life of great **penances** <term>Bußübungen: Physical or spiritual exercises, such as fasting or hair shirts, performed as acts of repentance or self-discipline.</term> so that he might be known and named for [his] great **holiness** and thereby earn a great reward. This is because all natural love is devoted to itself and gladly receives honor in this time and a great reward in eternity. 

Another person has many wishes and asks for and desires all sorts of special graces from God. Such people are often deceived, for sometimes the things they desire are granted to them through the interference of the **Enemy** <note>A common historical reference to the devil or demonic forces.</note>. They then attribute this to their own holiness and believe they are worthy of all of it; for they are **haughty** <term>Hoffärtig: A traditional term for extreme pride or spiritual arrogance, considering oneself superior in the eyes of God.</term> and neither touched nor enlightened by God. Because of this, they do not transcend themselves, and even a small comfort can throw them into great joy, for they have no inkling of what is being withheld from them. And their desire is entirely [directed] toward the...

### 76. Concerning those who live contrary to the third way and to all virtues.

In mystical theology, this refers to the state where the soul has lost its resemblance to God through sin and self-centeredness.</note>. And here the third opposition begins, which is the most harmful of all: this is a life of **injustice**, full of spiritual errors and all perversity. ❱ Now consider the following attentively, so that—

<meta>This page continues the critique of heterodox mystics who claim a state of spiritual "vacancy." The previous page warned that these individuals believe they can be united with God without the need for love (Minne) or virtue. This page specifically addresses their contradictory claims regarding God's power and their own ability to earn "merit" through a state of total passivity.</meta>

[They confess] that He is infinitely powerful and rich, yet they claim that He can neither give to them nor take from them, and that for them there is no longer any progress or **merit**. And at times they claim the exact opposite, saying that they earn a greater reward than other people because God performs their works, and that they—without any cooperation—endure God’s **activity** and are "worked" by Him themselves; in this, they say, the highest merit consists. 

But this is all a deception and [utterly] impossible. For God’s **activity** is in itself eternal and unchangeable, for He works within Himself and nothing else. And within this activity, neither the progress nor the merit of any creature is included, for here there is nothing but God, who can become neither greater nor lesser. 

Creatures, however, have their own **activity**, through the power of God in nature, in **grace**, and in **glory**; and if these works end here below in grace, they continue eternally in glory. Now, if it were possible—which it is not—for the **spiritual creature** <note>The human soul or angelic being.</note> to be annihilated in its works and become as empty <note>original: *entledigt*. Referring back to the state of "vacancy" or "emptiness" (Ledigkeit) discussed on the previous page.</note> as it was before it existed—that is, that it became one with God just as it was then in its [archetypal] <note>original: *urbildlich*. Refers to the Neoplatonic idea that all things exist eternally as "ideas" or "archetypes" in the mind of God before they are created in time.</note> state—then it could earn nothing, just as it could not then. 

In that case, the creature would be no holier or more **blessed** than a stone or a piece of wood; for without our own activity—without us ourselves loving and knowing God—we cannot be **blessed** <term>Blessed: *Selig*, referring to the state of eternal happiness or beatitude found in the presence of God.</term>. God, however, would remain blessed just as He has been from **eternity**; but that could [avail] us nothing...

<summary>The author refutes the claim that humans can earn merit through total passivity, arguing that blessedness requires the soul's active participation in loving and knowing God. He clarifies the distinction between God's eternal, internal activity and the grace-assisted actions of created beings.</summary>
<keywords>merit, activity of God, grace, glory, spiritual creature, blessedness, archetypal state, spiritual passivity, mysticism</keywords>

--- Page 196 ---
<meta>This page continues the refutation of "false vacancy." The previous sentence concluded that even if a creature could be annihilated back into its archetypal state in God, it would be of no use or benefit to the soul's salvation.</meta>

...be of use. And therefore, all this talk of "vacancy" <note>original: *Ledigkeit*. A state of total passivity or "emptiness" claimed by certain heterodox mystical groups, such as the Brethren of the Free Spirit, who believed they could transcend the need for morality.</note> is a deception; for they seek to cast all wickedness and perversion in a different light, presenting them as nobler and higher than any virtue. They wish to clothe the worst things in a beautiful appearance, so that they might appear to be the best. Such people are in opposition to God and all His saints; indeed, they bear an exceeding resemblance to the damned spirits in hell, for those damned spirits are also without love <note>original: *Minne*. A specific medieval term for the soul's intense, reciprocal love for God.</note> and knowledge. They too are empty of gratitude, of praise, and of any loving "turning-toward" <note>The intentional directing of the soul's will and affection toward the Creator.</note> God; and this is the reason that they remain eternally damned. The only thing these people lack is for their temporal life to pass into eternity, at which point the justice of their works shall be made manifest.

### D 

But Christ, the Son of God, in His humanity, is the head of all good people, as well as the <term>standard (Richtschnur): literally a "measuring line" used by builders; here it means the perfect moral example</term> by which we ought to live. He was, and is, and remains forever—together with all His members, which is to say all the saints—full of love, desire, gratitude, and praise toward His heavenly Father. Furthermore, His soul was and is united and blessed within the divine essence; yet, He could never arrive at that state of "vacancy," nor shall He ever do so. For His glorified soul, like those of all the blessed, turns eternally toward God in love, just like those who hunger and thirst and have tasted God, yet can never be satisfied. Meanwhile, this same soul of Christ and all the saints rest above all desire in the enjoyment of God, where there is only the One. This is eternal blessedness <note>original: *Seligkeit*. The state of supreme happiness and spiritual fulfillment in the presence of God.</note>.

<summary>The author continues to condemn the "vacancy" of false mystics, comparing their lack of love to the state of the damned, and contrasts this with the true, active love and eternal desire found in Christ and the saints.</summary>
<keywords>vacancy, Ledigkeit, virtue, damnation, Minne, Christ, humanity of Christ, eternal hunger, beatific vision, blessedness</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the argument against the "false vacancy" of the soul, contrasting it with the active, loving life of Christ and the saints. The text transitions from a polemic against heresy to a description of the ideal mystical life of the soul.</meta>

of God and all his <term>chosen ones (Auserwählten): those called or elect in the eyes of God</term>. ❡ Thus the **blessedness** of Christ and his saints consists in both enjoyment and activity, which is also the life of all good people, according to the measure of the love belonging to each. And this is a **righteousness** that shall never pass away. And therefore we should adorn ourselves outwardly and inwardly with **virtues** and good **customs**, just as the saints did. 

And we should present ourselves lovingly <note>original: *minniglich*. A term derived from "Minne," the intense, reciprocal love between the soul and God.</note> and humbly before the eyes of God in all our works: then we shall encounter God through the mediation of all his gifts. And then we shall be touched by a felt love and filled with goodness toward all people. 

And so we shall be "flowing out" and "flowing back" in righteous love, and established and permanently abiding in simple peace and in **God-likeness**. And through this likeness, through "enjoying love" and divine clarity, we flow out of ourselves into the unity and encounter God through God directly in "enjoying rest" <note>This describes the "common life," a core concept for the author where the soul simultaneously rests in God and acts in the world.</note>. And so we reach the point that we always remain within, always flow out, and without ceasing return home again. And by this, we possess a true, inward life in all **perfection**. That this may happen to us, may God help us. Amen.

<summary>The text concludes that true spiritual blessedness requires a balance of resting in God and active, virtuous living. It describes the mystical cycle of the soul "flowing out" to do good works and "flowing back" into divine unity.</summary>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Book 3 of the treatise. It shifts focus from the "active" and "inward" lives discussed in the previous books to the highest stage of mystical experience: the "contemplative" or "God-beholding" life.</meta>

# Here begins the third book of The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage.

1. How one attains a **God-beholding life** through three things. The inward lover of God, who possesses God in **enjoying rest** <note>original: *genießendem Ruhen*. This refers to "fruition," a state of peaceful, ecstatic enjoyment of God's presence.</note>, themselves in devoted, active love, and their whole life in virtues according to righteousness—such an inward person attains a **contemplative life** through these three qualities and through the secret revelation of God. 

Yes, it is such an inward and righteous lover whom God wishes to choose of His own free will and elevate to a **super-essential vision** <note>original: *überwesentlichen Schauen*. A technical term for a type of seeing that transcends human reason and created "essence" (being), reaching into the divine nature itself.</note>, in divine light and in the manner of God. This vision transports us into a purity and clarity beyond all our understanding, for it is a special **adornment** and a heavenly crown, as well as the eternal reward of all virtues and all life. 

And no one can attain this through science or cleverness <note>original: *Scharfsinn*. Intellectual sharpness or analytical reasoning.</note>, nor by any self-led exercise. Rather, only those whom God wishes to unite with His Spirit and illuminate through Himself can behold God, and no one else. The hidden **divine nature** is eternally beholding and loving <note>original: *minnend*. Derived from "Minne," a high-medieval term for a deep, reciprocal spiritual love.</note> in an active way according to the distinction of the Persons <note>This refers to the Holy Trinity: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit acting as distinct persons.</note> and is always "enjoying" in an embrace of the Persons within the unity of their essence.

<summary>This page introduces the third book, focusing on the "God-beholding" life. It explains that this contemplative state is a gift from God granted to those who balance active virtue with inward devotion.</summary>
<keywords>contemplative life, super-essential vision, divine nature, Trinity, spiritual marriage, fruition, Ruusbroec</keywords>

¹ How Ruysbroeck wishes these and similar passages of his book to be understood is perhaps best seen from the following section of his work, *The Twelve Beguines* (Chapter 14: On the Fourfold Love): “Next follows the third way, which is that of a clarified love, raised up into the divine light. In this state, spirits are empty and bare, caught up beyond all activity in a state of pure knowing and pure love. They do not act themselves, but are driven and acted upon by the Spirit of the Lord; in their own **perception** <note>original: *Empfinden*</note>, they have themselves become grace and love. They are called **sons of God**, for they have died to themselves in God and have immersed all their individuality into the most beloved will of God. Their life is hidden with Christ in God, and they are born without ceasing from the Holy Spirit as chosen sons of the love of God. And beyond grace and beyond all their works, they are fit to be annihilated and melted down in love; for they are **God-formed** <note>original: *gottförmig*. As far as there is iron, there is fire; and as far as there is fire, there is iron. Yet the iron does not become fire, and the fire does not become iron, but each retains its own substance and nature. In the same way, the spirit of man does not become God, but he is God-formed, and he feels that he is 'breadth, length, height, and depth.' And as far as God is God, so far is the loving spirit united with God in love.”

### 2. How one must practice if they wish to receive the eternal light and behold God.

If the spirit is now to behold God immediately through God in this divine light, then the human being necessarily requires three things. ☽ The first is that he be well-ordered from without in all virtues, and unhindered from within and so free from all external activity, just as if he were not active at all; for if he is occupied from within by any **work of virtue** <note>original: *Tugendwerk*. This refers to the performance of good deeds, which, while positive, can still preoccupy the mind and prevent the total stillness required for contemplation.</note>, then he carries an **image** <note>original: *Bild*. In mystical theology, an "image" is any mental construct or concept that stands between the soul and the formless reality of God.</note> within himself, and as long as that has not vanished within him, he cannot behold [God]. ☽ Second, he must cling to God from within with a love-burning intention and **Minne** <term>original: *Minne*. A specific term for a deep, courtly, and spiritualized love, used here to describe the soul's passionate longing for the Divine.</term>, just like an ignited, glowing, inextinguishable fire. As long as he feels himself in this state, he can behold [God]. ☽ Third, he must have lost himself in a **wayless-way** <note>original: *Unweise*. A state of being that is beyond all human methods, modes, or "ways" of understanding; a "mode-less" existence.</note> and a darkness, in which all **beholders of God** <note>original: *Gottschauer*. Those who have attained the state of mystical contemplation.</note> have lost their way in enjoyment, and in which they can never find themselves again in a **creaturely manner** <note>original: *geschöpflicherweise*. From the perspective of a finite, created being.</note>. In the abyss of this darkness, where the loving spirit has died to itself, the **revelation of God** and eternal life begin. For in that darkness, an incomprehensible light shines and is born; that is the Son

## 4. How our spirit is summoned to go out into beholding and enjoyment.

Now the Spirit of God speaks in the hidden **melting-away** <term>original: *Zerflossenheit*. A mystical state where the soul's individual boundaries seem to dissolve into the divine presence.</term> of our spirit: “Go out,” into an eternal beholding and enjoyment in the manner of God. All the wealth that is in God by nature, we possess through love in God and God in us, by virtue of the immeasurable love which is the Holy Spirit; for in this love one tastes everything that one can desire. And therefore, through this very love, we have died to ourselves and gone out in **loving** <note>original: *minniglicher*. Derived from *Minne*, a term for sacred, ecstatic love common in medieval German and Dutch mysticism.</note> melting-away, in **un-way** <term>original: *Unweise*. A state beyond specific modes, methods, or human understanding; a "wayless" state of being.</term> and in darkness. There the spirit—*in the embrace of the holy Trinity*—remains eternally dwelling within the **super-essential** <term>original: *überwesentlichen*. Referring to a reality that exists beyond all created being or essence, often used to describe the nature of God.</term> unity, in rest and blessed enjoyment. And in this same unity, corresponding to its fruitfulness, the Father is in the Son, and the Son in the Father, and all creatures are in them both. And this unity is above the distinction of the persons; for here there is only for the intellect a perception of **fatherhood and sonship** <note>The internal relations of the Trinity (Father and Son) as understood by the human mind.</note> in the living fruitfulness of the divine nature.

God’s image and likeness, and our image and likeness; for in this, God reflects Himself and all things. In this divine image, all creatures possess an eternal life apart from their own selfhood <note>Meaning their existence as distinct, time-bound individuals.</note>, as if in their eternal **archetype** <term>original: *Urbild*. The primordial model or "idea" of a thing existing in the mind of God.</term>; and according to this eternal image and this likeness, the **Holy Trinity** has created us. Therefore, God wills that we "go out" from ourselves into this divine light and supernaturally strive for this image—which is our own true life—and possess it with Him, both active and enjoying in eternal **blessedness** <term>original: *Seligkeit*. The state of supreme happiness and union with God.</term>.

» For we clearly perceive that the **bosom of the Father** <note>The deepest, most fertile source of the Godhead from which the Son is born.</note> is our own **ground** <term>original: *Grund*. The foundational essence of the soul where it meets God.</term> and our origin, wherein we begin our life and our being. And from our own ground—that is, from the Father and from everything that lives in Him—an eternal clarity shines forth; this is the **birth of the Son**. And in this clarity—that is, in the Son—the Father recognizes Himself and all that lives in Him; for everything He is and everything He has, He gives to the Son, save only for the property of **fatherhood**, which He remains in Himself. 

Thus, everything that lives hidden in the Father within that Unity now lives openly, having "flowed forth" <term>original: *ausgeflossen*. The manifestation of the divine essence into the distinct person of the Son.</term> in the Son. And yet, the simple ground of our eternal image always remains in **darkness** <note>A "divine darkness" or "cloud of unknowing" that is beyond human reason.</note> and in a **modeless state** <term>original: *Unweise*. A state beyond all specific forms, manners, or "ways" of being.</term>. But the immeasurable clarity radiating from it reveals and brings the hiddenness of God to light in [manifold] ways. And all people who are lifted above their **creatureliness** <note>The state of being a created thing, as opposed to the uncreated nature of God.</note> into a **contemplative life** <term>original: *schauendes Leben*. A life characterized by the direct mystical "beholding" or vision of God.</term> are one with this divine clarity. Indeed, they are

this clarity itself, and they see, feel, and perceive within themselves by means of this divine light, that they are that same simple **ground** <term>original: *Grund*. The state of existing as an eternal "idea" in the mind of God before being brought into physical existence.</term>, out of which clarity radiates boundlessly in a divine manner, and in which it remains eternally according to the simplicity of the essence, simple and without mode <note>original: *ohne Weise*. This refers to a state of being that is beyond any specific form, method, or human description.</note>.

And therefore the inward, contemplative people should, according to the mode of contemplation, go out beyond reason and distinction and beyond their created essence in an eternal gazing. Through the indwelling light they are transformed and made one with that same light, through which and in which they see. And thus the contemplative people reach their eternal image, according to which they were made, and they behold God and all things, without distinction, in one simple vision in the divine light. 

And this is the noblest and most beneficial contemplation that one can attain in this life. For in this contemplation, man remains best of all master of himself and free, and can grow in the majesty of life through every **loving** <term>original: *minniglich*. Derived from *Minne*, a Middle High German/Dutch term for a profound, mystical, and reciprocal love between the soul and God.</term> inwardness, beyond all understanding. For he remains free and master of himself in inwardness and virtue. And this gazing into the divine light holds him above all inwardness and above all virtues and above all merit, for it is the crown and the reward for which we strive, and which we now have and possess in this manner [of contemplation]; for the contemplative life is a heavenly life. [the soul's] created nature <term>original: *Geschaffenheit*. The state of being a created being, as opposed to the uncreated essence found in God.</term> would be even more capable of receiving the light, and then God's glory could shine through us better and more nobly according to all modes. This is the mode beyond all modes, how one goes out into a divine contemplation and into an eternal gazing <note>original: *ewiges Starren*. This refers to a fixed, unwavering state of mystical focus where the soul is motionless in God.</note>, and how one is transformed and transfigured in divine light. This "going out" of the contemplative person is also loving; for through an enjoying love he grows beyond his created nature and finds and tastes the richness and the bliss which God Himself is, and which God pours out without ceasing into the hiddenness of the spirit, through which man resembles the nobility of God.

## 6. Concerning a divine encounter, which is accomplished in the hiddenness of the spirit.

As soon as the inward, contemplative person has thus reached his eternal image and has entered into the bosom of the Father through the Son in this purity, he is enlightened with divine truth and receives the **eternal birth** <note>original: *ewige Geburt*. A central mystical concept where the Son of God is born within the soul of the believer, renewing them continually.</note> anew every hour, and he goes out according to the mode of light into a divine contemplation. And here the fourth and last point begins: namely, a loving encounter, in which our highest, all-surpassing blessedness consists. You must know that the heavenly Father, as a **living ground** <term>original: *lebendiger Grund*. The absolute, essential depth of the Godhead from which all things flow.</term> with everything that lives in Him, is actively turned toward His Son as His own eternal wisdom. And the same wisdom, and everything that lives in it, is in turn actively turned toward the Father—that is, toward the same ground from which it proceeds.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 194 regarding the "loving encounter" between the soul and the Trinity. And in this encounter, the third person proceeds between the Father and the Son, namely the Holy Spirit, who is the love of them both and who is one with both in one and the same nature. And this Spirit embraces and permeates—both actively and through enjoyment—the Father and the Son and everything that lives in them both, with such great richness and such joy that all creation must remain eternally silent before this. For the incomprehensible wonder that lies in this love eternally surpasses the understanding of all creatures. But where one understands and tastes this wonder without amazement <note>In this context, "without amazement" suggests a state of direct, intuitive experience that has moved beyond the initial shock or cognitive "wonder" into a steady, mystical participation.</note>, there the spirit is ravished from itself and is one with God’s Spirit; it tastes and sees without measure, just as God does, the richness which is God Himself, in the unity of the living ground, where it possesses itself according to the mode of its **uncreatedness** <term>original: *Ungeschaffenheit*. The mystical concept of the soul's eternal essence as it exists in the mind of God, distinct from its existence as a created being in time.</term>.

Now this blissful encounter is actively renewed in us without ceasing, according to the way of God; for the Father gives Himself in the Son, and the Son in the Father, in an eternal delight and a **loving** <note>original: *minniglich*. Rooted in the Middle High German *Minne*, this describes a deep, reciprocal, and often ecstatic spiritual love.</note> embrace, and this is renewed every hour in the bond of love. For just as the Father, without ceasing, gazes upon all things anew in the birth of His Son, so all things are loved anew by the Father and the Son in the outflowing of the Holy Spirit. And this is the active encounter of the Father and the Son, in which we are lovingly embraced by the Holy Spirit in eternal love. Now this active encounter and this loving embrace are, in their ground, enjoyable and **without mode** <note>original: *ohne Weise*. This refers to an experience of God that transcends all human methods, images, or finite descriptions—a direct union with the Divine Essence.</note>;

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "loving encounter" between the soul and the Trinity. ...for the abyssal un-manner <note>original: *Unweise*. A technical mystical term referring to God's mode of being that transcends all human categories, forms, or "ways."</note> of God is so dark and mode-less that it contains all divine modes, all activity, and the attributes of the [Divine] Persons within the rich embrace of the essential unity, and produces a divine enjoyment in the abyss of unutterability. And here a blissful passing-over and a dissolving immersion takes place in the essential nakedness, where all divine names, all modes, and all living ideas—which are reflected as images in the mirror of divine truth—fall into the simple namelessness, into un-manner and indeterminacy. For in this bottomless whirlpool <note>original: *Strudel*. A common metaphor in Rhenish-Flemish mysticism for the overwhelming, drawing power of the Godhead that "swallows" the individual self.</note> of simplicity, all things are embraced in blissful enjoyment, and the <gloss>essential</gloss> ground itself remains completely misunderstood, unless [it be] by the essential unity <gloss>itself</gloss>. 

Before this, the Persons and everything that lives in God must give way; for here there is nothing but an eternal resting in a blissful embrace of **loving dissolution** <note>original: *minniglicher Zerflossenheit*. This describes the soul "melting" into God through the heat of spiritual love (*Minne*), losing its rigid boundaries.</note>. And that is the mode-less essence, chosen above all things by all inward spirits. That is the dark silence in which all lovers are lost. But if we could thus prepare ourselves in virtues—we would almost strip ourselves of the body and flow away into the wild waves of the sea, and nevermore would a creature be able to catch us. That we may possess the essential unity in enjoyment and clearly behold the Unity in the Trinity—may divine love, which turns no beggar away, grant us this. Amen.

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the "Afterword" by the German translator. The previous page concluded the primary mystical text of Ruysbroeck's "The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage," ending on the theme of the soul's dissolution into the divine silence. This page shifts to a scholarly discussion regarding other contemporary translations, specifically a French edition from 1920.</meta>

# Afterword.

The manuscript of this translation had already been handed over for printing when I came across the French translation of *The Adornment of the Spiritual Marriage* <note>original: *Zierde der geistlichen Hochzeit*. This is Ruysbroeck's most famous work, written in the 14th century.</note>, which had already been published by the Benedictines of St. Paul de Wisques in 1920.¹ It is the third volume of their edition of Ruysbroeck’s writings. I was already familiar with the first two volumes, but unfortunately, I did not learn of the publication of the third volume until now. While reviewing the printer's proofs, however, I still had the opportunity to take the translation of my French brothers into account.

Their edition of Ruysbroeck promises to be the most complete of all those published in translation so far. One senses at every turn that the translators have fully immersed themselves in Ruysbroeck’s world of thought. The general introduction, as well as the introductions to the individual writings and the footnotes, provide an excellent introduction to this world of thought and offer many details about the origin, distribution, and reception of his works. I cannot, however, hide the fact that in this French edition, as with Maeterlinck <note>Maurice Maeterlinck (1862–1949), the Belgian Nobel laureate whose 1891 translation of Ruysbroeck helped spark a modern mystical revival.</note>, I find Ruysbroeck’s warmth and intimacy somewhat lacking. One no longer senses much of the fragrance of the forests and flower gardens that breathes through the writings of our Blessed one <note>Ruysbroeck was beatified by the Catholic Church in 1908, shortly before this translation was likely produced.</note>, who loved to meditate, pray, and contemplate in the open air. One sometimes feels transported out of the solitude of the Groenendaal hermitage <note>The "Green Valley" monastery in the Sonian Forest near Brussels where Ruysbroeck lived and wrote.</note> into a

---

¹ *Works of Ruysbroeck the Admirable* <note>original: *Oeuvres de Ruysbroeck l'admirable*</note>. Brussels, Vromont & Co.

<summary>In this afterword, the translator acknowledges a newly discovered French edition of Ruysbroeck's works by Benedictine monks, praising its scholarship while critiquing its lack of the "spiritual warmth" and natural atmosphere found in the original.</summary>
<keywords>afterword, Jan van Ruysbroeck, Benedictines of St. Paul de Wisques, Maurice Maeterlinck, Groenendaal, translation critique, mysticism</keywords>

--- Page 212 ---
<meta>This page continues the translator's afterword, transitioning from a critique of the French language's formal nature to a scholarly discussion of the dating and historical context of Ruysbroeck's major works, specifically "The Spiritual Marriage."</meta>

...lecture hall of the Sorbonne. This, however, is connected to what I said in the preface regarding the French language. Since the French edition is currently unaffordable for many, I would like to share here what my learned brothers say about the period in which *The Spiritual Marriage* was written.

According to their opinion, *The Spiritual Marriage* is very likely the second major work that Ruysbroeck composed. His first attempt was *The Kingdom of God's Beloved* <note>original: *Das Reich der Geliebten Gottes*</note>, which he did not publish. Perhaps he was not entirely satisfied with this work, or he feared it could be misunderstood. Only recently had Meister Eckhart <note>Meister Eckhart (c. 1260–1328) was a major German mystic whose work was partially condemned as heretical shortly after his death.</note> been condemned (March 27, 1329); since then, every new mystical writing was received with suspicion. 

However, the reason why Ruysbroeck had taken up the pen—combating the heresy of the "Brothers of the Free Spirit" <note>A lay movement that claimed a soul united with God was above the moral laws of the Church, a view Ruysbroeck strongly opposed.</note>, which was spreading more and more—remained. Thus, Ruysbroeck set to work once again and wrote *The Spiritual Marriage*, in which he treated the same material, only better organized and expanded. *The Kingdom of God's Beloved* was likely written around the year 1330, the year when the Bull *Benedictus Deus* regarding the "Beatific Vision" <term>Beatific Vision: from the Latin *Visio beatifica*, the immediate perception of God by the soul in heaven.</term> appeared. The learned monks of Wisques rightly assume that Ruysbroeck would have spoken more precisely about the difference between the vision of God here on earth and in heaven—as he later did in his *The Sparkling Stone* <note>original: *Handzierat*, literally "hand-adornment," but standardly known in English as *The Sparkling Stone*.</note>—had he written his main work after 1336. Ruysbroeck...

<summary>The translator discusses the historical context and dating of Ruysbroeck’s works, suggesting that "The Spiritual Marriage" was a revised response to the "Free Spirit" heresy and was likely written before 1336.</summary>
<keywords>Jan van Ruysbroeck, Meister Eckhart, The Spiritual Marriage, The Kingdom of God's Beloved, Benedictus Deus, Beatific Vision, Brothers of the Free Spirit, Wisques, dating of manuscripts</keywords>

...he was thus about forty years old and was still a chaplain at St. Gudula <note>The Cathedral of St. Michael and St. Gudula in Brussels, where Ruysbroeck served before retiring to a more contemplative life.</note> when he wrote *The Spiritual Marriage*. 𝔇 It is of particular importance to mention that this work was translated into Latin during Ruysbroeck's own lifetime by his fellow brother Wilhelm Jordaens, at the request of the Cistercians <note>A Roman Catholic order of enclosed monks.</note> of Ter Doest near Bruges. Furthermore, Thomas à Kempis <note>Thomas à Kempis (c. 1380–1471) was the author of "The Imitation of Christ" and a follower of the "Modern Devotion" movement influenced by Ruysbroeck.</note>, who was an indirect student of Ruysbroeck, likewise translated this masterpiece into Latin.
